->**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**<-
<note>original Latin: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA." Hermeticism is a philosophical and religious system based on the purported teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on the hidden relationships between the human soul, the natural world, and the divine.</note>

<image-desc>A circular emblem or bookplate pasted onto a marbled paper background. The emblem features a central oval illustration of a **pelican in her piety** (feeding her young with blood from her breast), a classic symbol of sacrifice and <term>alchemy<note>A historical tradition of natural philosophy and spiritual growth, famously represented by the attempt to turn lead into gold.</note></term>. The pelican sits atop a square pedestal decorated with four roses, a common symbol in <term>Rosicrucianism<note>A secret spiritual movement of the 17th and 18th centuries that sought to reform society through alchemical and mystical enlightenment; its name is derived from the "Rose Cross."</note></term>. Above the pelican, rays of light descend from a celestial source. The oval is bordered by a blue ring and surrounded by the circular inscription "**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**" in gold lettering.</image-desc>

# I.

## On the Origin of the Mysteries.

The origin of the mysteries <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian or Mithraic mysteries, which required initiation to join</note>, so highly respected throughout the entire ancient world, belongs to those enigmas that will hardly ever be solved with complete accuracy. Everything that has been said about them to this day, and everything that can be said, is all conjecture; and while one theory may have more plausibility than another, none provides sufficient certainty. The information found in the writings of the ancients on this subject sheds just as little light on these obscurities, even though they are the only sources from which we can draw. They provide us only with accounts of various transmissions of the mysteries from one people to another. In them, we read that **Cadmus** and **Inachus** introduced the mysteries to Greece, **Erichtheus** to Athens, **Trophonius** to Boeotia, **Melampus** to Argos, **Minos** to Crete, **Orpheus** to Thrace, and others to various other places.

<vocab>mysteries, Cadmus, Inachus, Erichtheus, Trophonius, Melampus, Minos, Orpheus, Greece, Athens, Boeotia, Argos, Crete, Thrace</vocab>

<summary>This opening section explores the elusive origins of ancient mystery religions, noting that while historical sources track their spread through legendary figures like Orpheus and Cadmus, their true beginnings remains a mystery.</summary>
<keywords>Origins of Mysteries, Ancient Greece, Mythology, Initiation, Cadmus, Orpheus, Cultural Transmission</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the origins of the ancient mysteries. It moves from listing legendary figures who spread these rites to questioning why their true beginnings remain obscured—suggesting that the history itself was a guarded secret.</meta>

These accounts show which individuals should be credited with introducing the mysteries into various lands; yet their true origin remains as unknown as ever. But are even these reports reliable? It may be that nothing definite or fully certain can ever be said regarding this subject. Perhaps the very accounts of the mysteries' origins were among those secrets not meant for public release, and about which only the fully initiated <note>The Russian "наставленные" refers to those who had been "instructed" or initiated into the secret teachings of a mystery cult</note> received knowledge. This seems highly probable to me. It was not only matters of great importance—not just the loftiest philosophy, theology, or whatever else it might be called—that comprised the subject of the ancient mysteries: history also belonged to them. Frequently, a single historical detail, which might be dismissed as an incidental trifle, explains other matters; and since the initiates did not wish to reveal or popularize the latter, it was necessary to keep the former hidden as well.

Furthermore, we find that nearly all mysteries of the ancient world were dedicated to specific deities, to whom their origins were attributed. The Egyptians traced their mysteries back to **Osiris** <note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife and rebirth</note> and **Isis** <note>The Egyptian goddess of healing and magic, wife of Osiris</note>.

-><vocab>mysteries, origin, sacred rites, philosophy, theology, Egyptians, Osiris, Isis</vocab><-

<summary>The author suggests that the true history of the mysteries was itself a secret guarded by initiates to prevent the exposure of deeper philosophical truths. It is noted that most ancient mysteries, such as those of Egypt, were attributed to specific gods like Osiris and Isis.</summary>
<keywords>Secret history, initiation, Egyptian mythology, Osiris, Isis, concealment of knowledge, ancient theology</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine origins attributed to various mysteries. While the previous page mentioned the Egyptian deities Osiris and Isis, this page transitions to the Greek tradition, specifically the Athenian rites of Ceres and the rites of Bacchus, while beginning a critique of modern theories that attempt to link these sophisticated ancient mysteries to the primitive rituals of Indigenous peoples in the Americas.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>

The Eleusinian mysteries were communicated to the Athenians by *Ceres* <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Demeter, the deity of agriculture and the harvest</note>, to whom they were also dedicated. Bacchus <note>The Roman name for Dionysus, the god of wine and religious ecstasy</note>, to whom the *Orgies* <note>In this context, "Orgies" refers to the secret, ecstatic ritual worship of Dionysus, rather than the modern definition of the word</note> were dedicated, was regarded as both their founder and institutor. It may be that these accounts were nothing other than hieroglyphs <note>The author uses "hieroglyphs" here metaphorically to mean symbolic codes or sacred puzzles used to veil information</note> concealing their true origin, which was revealed only to those to whom all the representations were explained in detail. What the Greeks relate regarding Ceres's establishment of their Eleusinian mysteries is so similar to priestly invention and fable [a sacred tale <term>ἱερὸς λόγος</term> <note>original Greek: "ἱερὸς λόγος" (hieros logos), a term for a "holy word" or myth used to justify or explain a religious ritual</note>] that one cannot doubt its mythological nature. The common people were satisfied with such stories. But it is not for the first time that matters of the greatest importance are hidden beneath such sacred rumors.

Some more recent writers have wished to derive the mysteries of ancient peoples from times of savagery. In America, even now, one finds among savage peoples a certain kind of mystery, consisting of various pious rites and games maintained by Jugglers <note>The Russian "Жонглеры" (Zhonglery) here reflects an 18th-century European term for Indigenous North American shamans or medicine men, whose rituals were often dismissed by observers as mere sleight-of-hand or trickery</note> and used during the initiation of someone into the rank of the Jugglers. But anyone who has some knowledge of the mysteries of present-day savages and the mysteries of the ancient world will find it very easy to be convinced that the latter can in no way be compared with the former, and such...

<meta>catchword: are [суть]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the mythological origins of the Eleusinian and Bacchic mysteries served as symbolic veils for the common people, while dismissing contemporary theories that attempt to equate these sophisticated ancient rites with the shamanic practices of Indigenous Americans.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the theory that ancient mysteries originated from primitive "savagery." It transitions from comparing Greek rites to Indigenous American rituals, asserting that the complexity and scientific knowledge found in ancient mysteries prove they belong to an advanced state of civilization rather than a state of ignorance. It also introduces the influential theory of William Warburton regarding the political origins of these rites.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>

...are properties that make it entirely impossible to derive them from times of savagery. Although they <note>Referring to the mysteries of the ancient world</note> had their strange rites in certain instances, they also possessed many useful, impressive, and—if one analyzes their symbolic meaning—highly significant ceremonies, which in no way can be compared to the empty games practiced among savages. 

But the most important thing distinguishing these from those is that which pertains to the sciences <note>In this historical context, "sciences" (nauki) refers broadly to organized knowledge, philosophy, and the arts, rather than just the modern natural sciences</note>, which were found everywhere in the mysteries of the ancients, and about which I shall later have the opportunity to speak in greater detail. This does not belong to those times in which nations were still in a state of unenlightenment, but rather presupposes a fairly high degree of development. And this was the essential and primary character of the mysteries <term>таинства</term> <note>original Russian: "таинства" (tainstva). This term is used interchangeably with "mysteries" to describe secret religious rites</note>, which were held in such high esteem in the ancient world.

Warburton <note>William Warburton (1698–1779) was an English bishop and scholar whose famous work argued that the ancient mysteries were designed by the state to promote morality</note>, who included a very fine discourse on the mysteries in his book entitled ***The Divine Legation of Moses***, traces their origin to legislators who, in his opinion, invented, established, and supported them. Certainly, no one can deny that the government was aware of the content of the mysteries, and that

<summary>The author argues that the intellectual and scientific depth of ancient mysteries distinguishes them from primitive rituals, citing William Warburton's theory that these mysteries were established by ancient legislators for social and political purposes.</summary>

<keywords>ancient mysteries, William Warburton, Divine Legation of Moses, enlightenment, social development, ritual symbolism, legislators</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Bishop William Warburton’s theory regarding the origins of secret religious rites. While the previous page established that these "mysteries" were too sophisticated to be the product of "primitive" people, this page addresses whether they were political inventions created by early governments to maintain social order.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<sig>A 3</sig>

...they <note>referring to the mysteries</note> were under its <note>the government's</note> special supervision and patronage. A society engaged in such matters that must be hidden from the government does not deserve to be tolerated in any way; moreover, the mysteries of the ancient world were closely linked with the government, and therefore the government had to take an active interest in them. However, nothing is more unjust than **Warburton’s** <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English scholar who argued that mysteries were state-sponsored tools for moral control</note> opinion that the mysteries of the ancient world were invented by legislators simply because they contained great truths about the immortality of the human soul, and about punishments and rewards <note>in the afterlife</note>.

The reasons with which he supports his opinion are also insufficient to prove this. He asserts first that the mysteries originated from Egypt, and refers to the testimony of **Herodotus** <note>A 5th-century BC Greek historian often called the "Father of History"</note> and **Diodorus Siculus** <note>A 1st-century BC Greek historian known for his monumental universal history, the *Bibliotheca historica*</note>. It is indisputably true that the Greek mysteries, and especially the Eleusinian <term>Элевзинскія</term> <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone</note> ones, originated from Egypt, and underwent only some transformations according to the taste of the Greeks and comparisons with the Greek deities to whom they were dedicated. This is confirmed almost unanimously by the Greeks themselves when speaking of the origin of their mysteries. Perhaps the Thracian, Cretan...

<summary>This page argues against the idea that ancient mysteries were state-invented tools for social control, while acknowledging their likely Egyptian origins and their eventual adaptation by the Greeks.</summary>
<keywords>William Warburton, mysteries, Egypt, Herodotus, Diodorus Siculus, Eleusinian mysteries, immortality of the soul, Greek religion</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against William Warburton's theory that the ancient mysteries were political inventions. The text flows from the previous page's discussion on the Egyptian origins of Greek rites, specifically mentioning the Phoenician influence.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

...-sian <note>original: "скія"; likely completing "Pelasgian" or another Greek regional group from the previous page</note> and Phoenician mysteries were drawn from this same source. It is also true that the Egyptian priests were indeed authentic interpreters of political laws, just like the priests of the Jews, whose government was established according to the Egyptian model <note>A common 18th-century view held that Moses, "learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians," adapted their administrative structures for the Israelites</note>. But from the fact that the priests, as the learned class in Egypt, were the interpreters of the law, it does not yet follow that the legislators were the founders and inventors of the mysteries. And finally, from where did Egypt receive its mysteries—a land which, by its natural position, must have been enlightened later than other Asian lands? <note>In the historical geography of this period, "Asia" often referred generally to the East, including the Levant and Mesopotamia</note> This question remains forever unanswered.

Another reason cited by **Warburton** is that all the sages who brought the mysteries out of Egypt and spread them in Asia, Greece, and Britain were kings and legislators, such as: **Zoroaster, Inachus, Orpheus, Melampus, Trophonius, Minos, Cinyras, Erichtheus, and the Druids.** <note>These figures represent a mix of historical and mythological founders of civilizations: Zoroaster (Persian prophet), Inachus (Argos), Orpheus (Thrace), Melampus (healer), Trophonius (hero/oracle), Minos (Crete), Cinyras (Cyprus), Erichtheus (Athens), and the Celtic priestly class</note> — However, regarding some of those mentioned here, it is not yet fully certain whether they held royal and legislative status. **Zoroaster** was neither a king nor a legislator. The precepts given by him to the Persians concerned only religion.

<summary>The author challenges the claim that ancient mystery religions were invented by political leaders for social control, questioning the chronology of Egyptian enlightenment and noting that many "founders" were religious rather than political figures.</summary>
<keywords>William Warburton, Egyptian priests, Jewish government, Zoroaster, ancient mysteries, religious origins, legislators</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of William Warburton’s theory regarding the "political" invention of the ancient mysteries. The previous page questioned whether Egypt was the ultimate source of all wisdom and began listing historical and mythological figures that Warburton claimed were legislator-founders of these secret rites.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<sig>А 4</sig>

**Cinyras** <note>Cinyras was the mythical king of Cyprus and a priest of Aphrodite. The author uses his dual role to challenge the idea that mysteries were purely political inventions</note> in his own person combined the office of priest with the dignity of King, and therefore it is further asked: was it the priest, or the King, who introduced the mysteries to Cyprus? **Melampus** <note>A legendary seer and healer; according to Herodotus, he introduced the rituals of Dionysus to Greece</note> was not a King, nor a prophet, just as **Orpheus**, **Trophonius**, and the **Druids** <note>The author groups these figures to show that the founders of mysteries were often religious or mystical figures rather than secular rulers</note> were not. But let us suppose that this was even as Warburton says; however, it proves only that the mysteries of the ancient world, wherever they were established, were from their very introduction always under the protection of leaders and laws, and not that Kings and legislators invented them.

Warburton’s final reason is that the government presided over the mysteries. — This was, at the very least, only true of the Eleusinian Mysteries <note>The most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone at Eleusis</note>. Yet even if this were universal, it proves nothing more than that the government oversaw such assemblies so that they would not become corrupted or harmful to civil society; or that they were under the clear protection of the people's leaders.

Speaking of the introduction of mysteries among ancient peoples, one can say with certainty only that from their beginning they were under the protection of the laws. As for the rest, brought forward by Warburton in defense of his posi- <note>The Russian text breaks the word "положенія" (position/thesis) at the end of the page. Catchword: же-</note>

<summary>The author argues that while ancient governments protected and regulated religious mysteries to ensure social order, this does not prove that kings or legislators were the original inventors of these spiritual rites.</summary>
<keywords>Cinyras, Melampus, William Warburton, Eleusinian Mysteries, religious history, political philosophy, Druids</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of William Warburton’s "The Divine Legation of Moses," specifically addressing the origins of the ancient mysteries. The text picks up from the previous page's discussion of why civil authorities protected these rites, concluding a word started on page 7 (catchword: могу-).</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

...arguments <note>The word fragment "женія" likely completes "разсужденія" (arguments/reasonings) or "заключенія" (conclusions) from the previous page.</note> drawn from the nature of the mysteries—to which no slave or foreigner was admitted, and which required a virtuous life and exerted a beneficial influence on governance—only prove their excellence. They reveal the reasons why rulers might take them under their protection, but they do not make Kings and legislators their founders. Likewise, the passage he cites from **Plutarch** <note>Plutarch was a Greek philosopher and priest at Delphi whose writings on Isis and Osiris were frequently used by 18th-century scholars to analyze mystery traditions.</note> does not pertain to this. Thus, the history of the true origin of the mysteries is always lost in the deep gloom of antiquity, and remains hidden from the searching gaze of even the most industrious researcher who undertakes to present it before the eyes of the world. Although we find no sufficient reason to doubt the account of the Greeks, who derive the origin of their mysteries from **Egypt**, nevertheless the more detailed circumstances of their introduction are lost in the darkness of mythology <term>fable-talk (баснословіе) <note>This term refers to the era of myths and legends before recorded history.</note></term> and sacred rumors, just as is the question of where the Egyptians obtained their secrets, which then passed from them to others.

Amidst the universal silence of the ancients, it would be quite daring to venture to speak of this as an indubitable truth. And all that can be said are only probabilities, only hints <note>original: 'мановенія'—literally 'nods' or 'gestures', implying subtle indications or fleeting glimpses of truth.</note>,

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 8, transitioning from the uncertainty of historical records to the author's theory regarding the internal content of the ancient mysteries. It maintains the critique of the development of religious systems.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<sig>A 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: ta-</meta>

...which may, however, serve others by pointing out a path along which they may proceed further, should they deem it useful.

First of all, I must touch upon the main subject of the mysteries <term>mysteries (таинства) <note>While the text uses both "таинства" and "мистеріи," they both refer to the secret religious rites of antiquity, such as the Eleusinian or Isiac mysteries.</note></term> of the ancient world. Their essential characteristic was the affirmation of certain teachings contrary to the dominant religion followed by the common folk. The dominant religion of all peoples among whom mysteries were found—such as the Egyptians and the Greeks—was polytheism. Within the mysteries, an entirely different religion was taught—or philosophy, or whatever one prefers to call it. The original religion of the ancient world was not ***Polytheism***, but ***Deism***. <note>In the 18th-century context of this manuscript, "Deism" refers to "Natural Religion"—the belief that humanity originally possessed a simple, rational belief in one Creator before it was obscured by mythology.</note> Polytheism was a corruption <note>The original Russian "развратъ" (razvrat) here signifies a "perversion" or "distortion" of a pure state rather than its modern primary meaning of debauchery.</note> of it, produced partly by unskilled teaching about God and His attributes, partly by a distorted representation of the spiritual and physical worlds, partly by the dispersion of nations and the ensuing descent into barbarism, and partly by other circumstances; while the greed of priests nurtured and maintained this corruption until, at last, the original true religion was completely eradicated. It is probable that the origin of the mysteries should be sought in these early times, when there occurred...

<vocab>mysteries (таинства), mysteries (мистеріи), Egyptians, Greeks, polytheism (многобожіе), Polytheism (Полиѳеизмъ), Deism (Деизмъ), religion, priests</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that the ancient mysteries were established to preserve a "pure" original Deism (monotheism) in secret, protecting it from the "corrupted" polytheism practiced by the general public and exploited by priests.</summary>
<keywords>Deism, Polytheism, Ancient Mysteries, Religious Corruption, Egyptian Religion, Greek Religion, Natural Religion</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 9, explaining how the "true" original religion (Deism) was preserved in secret after the masses fell into Polytheism. It marks a transition from a general theory of religious decay to the specific case of the Ancient Greeks.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<meta>catchword: adopted (няли)</meta>

...such a great change in the religious system of all peoples, when they transitioned from ***Deism*** to ***Polytheism***. <note>The author is completing the thought from the previous page: that the shift from a rational belief in one God to the worship of many was a fundamental turning point in human history.</note> It seemed unforgivable to completely destroy and consign to oblivion this teaching, so innate to human reason. Superstition, popular prejudices, and many other circumstances—including personal interests—did not allow for the expulsion of polytheism. And so, they did not hinder its progress, while simultaneously retaining ***monotheism***, albeit only within the narrow confines of the initiated priests. These priests were already regarded as a far superior class of men and as mediators between humanity and the Deity, and they did not hesitate to claim the truth for themselves as an exclusive benefit; <note>The original Russian "участное благо" implies a good shared only among a specific group—the "participants" or initiates—rather than the public.</note> meanwhile, the common people wandered in error.

It is easy to observe that the origin I attribute to the <term>mysteries (мистеріи) <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world.</note></term> cannot be applied to all nations where they existed. At the least, it cannot be applied to the Greeks, from whose mysteries more has reached posterity than from any others. But it is also well known that the Greeks were not a people who fell from Deism into Polytheism, but rather a people who, with the help of philosophy, corrected their crude Polytheism. Furthermore, the mysteries were not their own invention; they borrowed...

In the times of **Abraham**, it seems, true religion was not yet entirely extinguished in Egypt. By the times of **Joseph**, the worship of the planets had already been introduced there. **Joseph** was wed to the daughter of **Pi-Ont-Phre** <note>The Egyptian name for Potipherah, mentioned in Genesis 41:45.</note>, a priest of the sun. And by the times of **Moses**, it seems this strange form of worship among the Egyptians had already reached its full extent. When they finally became acquainted with the Greeks, little by little, Greek deities were partly introduced into Egypt; and partly...

More recent writers thought to find a great similarity between the institutions of the **Egyptians** and the **Chinese**. **De Pauw** <note>Cornelius de Pauw (1739–1799), a Dutch diplomat and scholar who challenged the then-popular "Sinomania" (obsession with China) in Europe.</note> very effectively challenged this prejudice, although his representations of **China** are not well-founded in every respect. In this way, a thought that is true in itself can give birth to an obvious error.

## II.

### The Respect Accorded to the Mysteries by the Ancients.

In these inquiries, it is impossible to proceed even a single step further without constantly lamenting the lack of necessary information. Nevertheless, one can conclude with sufficient certainty that the esteem in which the Mysteries <note>The "Mysteries" refers to secret religious rites of the ancient world—such as the Eleusinian or Mithraic Mysteries—which were restricted to initiated members and often promised spiritual transformation.</note> were held among the ancients was remarkably great.

I do not wish to deny that the mysterious gloom, which shrouded everything within them, played a significant role in this reverence. While it often happens that unknown things are judged unfavorably, one need only present them in a certain light to make them objects of universal respect and wonder. Thus, the prudence of those who maintained the Mysteries knew how to arrange matters so that these rites, as the most exalted of <term>sacred things</term> <note>The original Russian *svyatyni* refers to holy objects, shrines, or deeply held religious values.</note>, were held in great honor and respect even by those to whom access was strictly forbidden. To this was added the fact that the Mysteries were conducted by priests who were favored by a positive prejudice—the belief that they possessed a most exceptional holiness and were entrusted with...

<summary>The author discusses the immense social and religious prestige of ancient Mystery cults, arguing that their secrecy and the perceived sanctity of the priesthood commanded respect even from the uninitiated.</summary>
<keywords>Mysteries, ancient religion, priesthood, secrecy, sacred rites, social status</keywords>

...communion with the Divine. Furthermore, not even all the priests participated in the mysteries, but only the most noble among them, who were recognized as worthy through various trials. Among certain peoples, and especially the Egyptians—where the priestly rank was <term>hereditary</term> <note>The original Russian "совокупленъ былъ съ нѣкоторыми фамиліями" literally means "joined with certain families," referring to the closed caste system of the Egyptian priesthood.</note>—only these families had access to the mysteries, to the exclusion of all the other people. The mysteries were also held under the special protection of religion and were themselves regarded as its holiest part—which indeed they were. For the outward religion, to which the common people clung, was the most foolish superstition imaginable, a strange mixture of all sorts of fables, many of which were of such a character that they could leave a harmful impression on public morals. In contrast, what was taught within the mysteries was, in its essence, the doctrine of sound reason; in some respects, one might even call it Divine truth, conveyed alongside virtue and significant teachings and precepts. Finally, something external was added to this. Although the inner substance was hidden from the common people, certain outward ceremonies and rites were not kept secret. And these, belonging to the holiest parts of religion, generally

The mysterious gloom that hid the mysteries from all outsiders, the priests who maintained them to the exclusion of all other people, and the opinion that they were the holiest part of the entire religion, excited universal respect for them; but no less was this respect caused by their outward magnificence. Humans always remain humans—that is, creatures accustomed to the sensory <note>original: "чувственному"; referring to things perceived through the physical senses rather than pure intellect.</note>; and it is quite impossible for objects that touch the senses in such a way not to simultaneously make an impression on the heart.

But as much as the mysteries were respected by outsiders, they were respected just as much by the initiates <term>initiates</term> <note>those who had been formally admitted into the secret knowledge of the mysteries.</note>. This can be concluded with sufficient certainty from the great caution with which they were hidden from everyone who did not belong to the priestly lineage <note>original: "поколенію"; here implying a hereditary caste.</note>. The great contradiction that existed between the mysteries and the popular religion undoubtedly played a significant role in this concealment. This popular religion had so taken hold of the minds of the people, and over time was so closely linked with the entire system of government, that it was impossible to make the mysteries public without the society undergoing significant changes. But without doubt, a significant part was also played by the great respect they had for...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the exclusion of the uninitiated from sacred mysteries. While the previous page touched on the secrecy of the Brahmins and Egyptians, this page transitions into a philosophical critique of why the priesthood held a monopoly on knowledge and introduces the Greek perspective.</meta>

...and the highest part of the mysteries was always reserved for the most select priests.

I do not wish to examine whether this is just or in accordance with the universal equality of men as confirmed by nature. Can one only be most capable and virtuous while in the service of religion; or can one also be capable and virtuous while belonging to the class of so-called laypeople <term>laypeople: individuals who are not members of the clergy or priesthood</term>? Perhaps this occurred because the priests, from the very beginning, sought to claim all learning for themselves to the exclusion of the rest of the people, just as was later the case among Christians in the Middle Ages. Among the Egyptians, all scholars, physicians, and legal experts generally belonged to the priesthood. This practice passed from them to the Jews. But it may also be that the first inventors of the mysteries were ministers of religion. The plausible original source of the sacraments <note>The Russian word "таинствъ" (tainstv) can refer to both religious sacraments and "mysteries" in the Greco-Roman sense.</note> that I have proposed confirms this. And so, over time, they were deemed inaccessible to laypeople. But however it may have been, everything serves as proof of the great reverence and respect in which the mysteries were held by those who maintained them.

Among the ancient Greek writers, we find more about the mysteries than anywhere else. Although they speak mostly only of

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ancient mysteries, specifically the Eleusinian Mysteries of Greece. This page argues for the underlying unity of all ancient mysteries.</meta>

the Eleusinian Mysteries, one can certainly assume that other mysteries were similar to these, and differed from them only in certain forms <note>original: "образованіями" (obrazovaniyami), likely meaning rituals or external structures</note>. If their origin did not already prove this—since they were all derived from a single source—one could also conclude it from other circumstances. *Herodotus* <note>original: "Иродотъ" (Irodot)</note> speaks in some places of the Egyptian mysteries just as others speak of the Eleusinian ones. The description of *Apuleius* <note>The author refers to the Roman writer's account of the initiation into the mysteries of Isis</note>, however mysterious it may be, is completely similar to what the Greeks occasionally mention regarding the Eleusinian mysteries, as a connoisseur can quickly notice (*). And these mysteries were held in the greatest esteem among the Greeks, Romans, and Barbarians <note>In this context, "Barbarians" refers to any non-Greek peoples, such as the Persians or Scythians</note>.

The opinion of the Greeks regarding both the Lesser and the Greater Mysteries <term>Lesser and Greater Mysteries: the two stages of the Eleusinian initiation; the Lesser was a prerequisite for the Greater</term> was such that from it one can recognize the great reverence in which they were held everywhere among them. *Whoever was initiated, the Athenians believed, and received instruction in the mysteries, became a participant in Divine honor after death.* Thus explains, at least, the Scholiast of *Aristophanes* <term>Scholiast: an ancient commentator or annotator who wrote explanations in the margins of classical texts</term>. This was not only in a moral sense—because the mysteries constituted the holiest part of religion, and those initiated into them belonged more to the deity than the rest of mortals—but also in a physical sense, for they thought that through consecrations and purifications both the body and the soul were cleansed.

But these advantages, which were hoped for from initiation into the mysteries, were not limited to temporal life alone. In the pagan world, there was a universal opinion that from these one could undoubtedly hope for a much better future life. At that time, it was thought that the souls of the initiated, upon death, were freed from all bonds and were led to the perfect enjoyment of bliss; while others, on the contrary, remained for a long time in mud and filth <note>The "mire" or "mud" is a common motif in ancient descriptions of the afterlife for the uninitiated, famously mentioned by Plato and in Orphic traditions</note>, until finally, after various transformations, they reached a state of bliss (*). Into the Lesser Mysteries, as is known, everyone was initiated; and he was not considered a good citizen who had not been initiated into them. However, these truths were not being declared for the first time in the <term>Lesser Mysteries</term> <note>The Lesser Mysteries were held annually in the spring at Agrai (near Athens) as a preliminary stage to prepare initiates for the more profound "Greater Mysteries" held in the autumn.</note>, and one cannot conclude that they were entirely unknown to the uninitiated. They were already contained in the mythological teachings of the poets and in the general folk religion; in the mysteries, they were merely made more impactful through dramatic performances. 

Initiation into these mysteries was, in the pagan world, in some way the same as <term>Chrismation</term> <note>In the Eastern Orthodox tradition, the sacrament following baptism that confers the gift of the Holy Spirit; it is similar in purpose to Confirmation in Western Christian traditions.</note> and confirmation are in the Christian world. Through them, people became—if one may put it this way—true members of the "pagan church," and by the most sacred obligations, they were bound to a virtuous life, which was meant to pave their path to future bliss. Thus, it is easy to see why every Greek strove for initiation into the mysteries, and why even the civil laws themselves were concerned with them. From this, it is also clear how great the respect was in the pagan world for both the Lesser and the Greater Mysteries.

Another proof of the extraordinary respect in which the mysteries were held is the great caution observed regarding those who were to be granted access to them. It is indisputable that in <term>paganism</term> <note>The author uses this term (Russian: *yazychestvo*) to refer broadly to the polytheistic religions of the ancient Greeks and Romans.</note>, purity of character was required from all who approached temples, sacrifices, and liturgical rites. At the very least, I cannot recall a single instance of this. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the exclusivity and moral standards of the Ancient Greek mysteries, contrasting them with general religious practices. In all other liturgical acts, <term>ablutions</term> <note>Ritual washings or cleansings meant to remove spiritual impurity.</note> and purifications served to render transgressors innocent and capable of participating in the sacred acts of religion. For this reason, **Suetonius** relates of **Nero** that during his travels through Greece, he did not dare to be initiated into the mysteries (*). <note>Suetonius records that Nero, having murdered his mother Agrippina, felt his conscience was too burdened to face the herald's proclamation at the Eleusinian Mysteries, which commanded the "impious and wicked" to stay away.</note> For the same reason, **Pausanias** says of the Greeks that they gave the sacraments as much higher precedence over other liturgical acts as they exalted the Gods above the Heroes (**). <note>In Greek mythology, "Heroes" were often semi-divine mortals; the author suggests the mysteries were seen as belonging to a higher, more divine realm of experience than standard civic religion.</note>

All this serves as clear evidence of the immense respect in which the mysteries were held by the ancients. The same practice was observed for other offices; from this fact alone, one might conclude the extraordinary esteem in which the mysteries were held, even if other evidence did not already prove it sufficiently.

The mysteries maintained this universal respect for as long as they endured; even their eventual decline and the abuses often committed in later times could not entirely eradicate it. This high regard persisted long after **Christianity** became the dominant religion. ### III.
### Opinions of the Church Fathers regarding the mysteries.

Just as favorably as pagan writers discourse everywhere about the mysteries, so shameful are the depictions of them found in the writings of the Church Fathers. These generally regard the mysteries as childish toys, with which steadfast and important people should not occupy themselves. ***Clement*** of Alexandria <note>Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 AD) was a Christian theologian who, in his work *Protrepticus*, provided detailed—if mocking—descriptions of pagan rituals to encourage conversion to Christianity.</note> says that once the venerable objects, which were carried about in the mysteries as things of great importance and holiness, were finally revealed, they were seen to be the most ridiculous and childish trifles. *What is contained*, says this Church Father, *in their mysterious chests? For their shrines must be opened and their secrets declared. Is it not bread, small pyramids, combed wool, cakes, salt, and the image of a dragon that are found among the sacred things of Bacchus?* <note>Bacchus is the Roman name for Dionysus; Clement is likely referring to the *cista mystica*, a basket used to carry sacred items in Dionysian and Eleusinian processions.</note> Beyond this

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the shift in perspective regarding the Greek mysteries, contrasting the pagan belief in their necessity for happiness with the mockery of early Christian leaders like Clement of Alexandria.</summary>
<keywords>Pretextatus, Church Fathers, Clement of Alexandria, Bacchus, Dionysian mysteries, religious polemic, Valentinian I</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of items found in the sacred chests of the mysteries as described by Clement of Alexandria on the previous page, further developing the early Christian polemic against pagan rituals.</meta>

besides these, pomegranates, hearts, vines, and ivy, as well as cakes and poppy seeds. These are their sacred objects! The mysterious symbols of **Themis** <note>Themis was the titaness of divine law and order; however, Clement of Alexandria often attributes these symbols to the cult of Demeter or the Eleusinian mysteries in his critiques.</note> are: **origanum** <term>origanum: wild marjoram, an aromatic herb often used in ancient Greek rituals and medicine</term>, a lamp, a sword, and a woman's comb, which, according to their mystical and secret expression, are in reality female reproductive organs (*).

**Arnobius** <note>Arnobius of Sicca (died c. 330 AD) was an early Christian apologist who wrote *Against the Heathens* (Adversus Nationes) to defend his new faith.</note> reasons in the same way. *What things*, he says, *could we bring forth, and what mysteries could we expose to universal ridicule, if we were not restrained by the public religion and respect for the written word* (**). In general, wherever the mysteries are mentioned in the writings of the **Church Fathers**, one can take it as certain that they are presented in a far from honorable light; instead, that which seemed worthy of respect to the **pagans** is represented there as quite insignificant and ridiculous.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the reliability of the early Church Fathers' accounts of pagan mysteries. ...did the same happen with them? There is also no sufficient reason to dispute everything they say. It is true that none of them recount having been previously **initiated** <term>Initiation: The process of being formally admitted into the secret knowledge and rituals of a religious group or mystery cult.</term> themselves; if they had been, they would certainly not have remained silent about it, as it would have made their accusations more credible. They all speak with such conviction that it seems there is not the slightest cause to doubt their descriptions.

On the other hand, it is equally noticeable that such men among the heathens—who were themselves initiated into these mysteries and whose moral character is beyond reproach, and who would surely have seen how harmful an immoral religion could be for society—speak of these very same mysteries with the utmost respect and recognize them as schools of the highest wisdom and virtue. <meta>This page continues the debate regarding the historical reliability of early Christian accounts of pagan mysteries. The author is weighing the potential bias of the Church Fathers against the possibility that the initiates of these cults were genuinely seeing something of value, rather than being mere victims of religious delusion.</meta>

[...persisted] in favorable conceptions of the **mysteries** <term>Mysteries: Secret religious rites of ancient Greece and Rome, such as the Eleusinian or Mithraic mysteries, which were kept hidden from the uninitiated and promised spiritual benefits.</term>, as if they were the most sacred part of **paganism**; but in my opinion, this prejudice could not extend so far that for its sake one could recognize the most ridiculous and childish trifles as things of great importance, and regard madness and godlessness as wisdom and virtue. Even the lowest rabble could hardly be so mistaken. If anyone wishes to say that prejudices about religion produce the strangest and most unexpected phenomena, might it not be just as appropriate to apply this to the **Church Fathers** <note>The influential Christian teachers and writers of the first few centuries AD who helped define early Christian doctrine, often by contrasting it sharply with "pagan" practices.</note>, and say that while the pagans, out of religious prejudice, overpraised their mysteries, the former [the Fathers], out of the same prejudice, reviled them? Whichever way we turn here, we find great difficulties everywhere.

What is strangest of all is that the Church Fathers in other places give quite a lot of justice to these very mysteries they so revile. ### IV.

### *The Relation of the Mysteries to Philosophy.*

It is well known that the philosophers of the ancient world, just like the priests, had their own **Secret Discipline** <note>original: *Disciplinam arcani*</note>, according to which certain truths or opinions were offered to everyone without distinction, while others were communicated only to those who had closer access to the philosophers, and in some way were, or wished to be, in a scholarly rank. It can be said that they possessed a two-fold method of teaching, which made their entire body of learning a riddle, so that they, while speaking

It also begins exploring how ancient philosophers used similar levels of secrecy to divide their teachings between the public and their inner circles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how ancient philosophers used a two-fold method of teaching—one public and one private—to protect themselves and their ideas from religious persecution.</meta>

...about one thing, they meant another.

The reasons for this were various. It was thought that not all truths would be useful to the common people, or that they could easily be misused by them. For this reason, they often deceived the people with prejudices and quite diligently concealed the truth from them. Another reason was provided by the very religion of those times. In paganism <note>The author uses "paganism" to refer broadly to the non-Christian religions of Greco-Roman antiquity.</note>, dissenters were treated no less strictly than they were later among the Christians. Although there was no Inquisition, there were courts to which crimes against religion were presented. Such was the *Areopagus* <note>The Areopagus was the supreme court of Athens, responsible for trials involving religious impiety.</note> in *Athens*. The execution of *Socrates* serves as an example of pagan intolerance <note>original: *интолеранція* (intolerantia)</note>. ***Plato***, without a doubt, did not dare to say in Athens what he ventured to say in Sicily. Philosophical truths often bore such a resemblance to the teachings of the mysteries that the public teaching of them could undoubtedly have been deemed a betrayal of the latter. For this reason, Socrates never wished to be initiated into the mysteries. For when he proposed principles similar to the truths of internal religion <note>A term likely referring to a more personal, rational faith as opposed to external, ritualistic state religion.</note>, it was not possible to accuse him, as a person who had never been initiated, of having betrayed

The extraordinary respect paid in ancient times to all sciences—which were regarded as a privilege that did not belong to the lower classes of people at all—also played a significant role in this twofold method of instruction. This was especially true during those times when the teachings of philosophers, particularly among the Greeks, were entirely open and anyone was permitted to attend; for naturally, only those truths thought not to belong to the common person were taught in secret.

In the most ancient times, this twofold method of instruction was nowhere more common or more extensively applied than among the **Pythagoreans** <note>The followers of Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BCE), who lived in a communal, secretive society and distinguished between "exoteric" students (listeners) and "esoteric" students (initiates).</note>. To the latter, he gave only moral precepts, while to the former, he revealed the highest truths of his philosophy. This was also the case with the **Alexandrians**. In their writings, they also frequently speak of the "mysteries of philosophy."

**Warburton** <note>William Warburton (1698–1779) was an English bishop and social critic. The whole question—whether the **mysteries** <note>original: *таинства* — in this context, the secret religious rites of antiquity.</note> were identical to **philosophy** and its truths—can be neither directly confirmed nor denied, as will soon be perceived by anyone who possesses a somewhat accurate knowledge of both. In this regard, it is necessary to judge both the various types of mysteries of the ancient world and the various philosophical sects. Some mysteries certainly cannot boast of that great advantage: that they contained matters of importance. This refers to all those which can, in some way, be considered derivative. Such, it seems, were the **Sacra Bonæ Deæ** <note>original: *Sacra Bonæ Deæ* (Holy Rites of the Good Goddess) — A Roman mystery rite reserved exclusively for women.</note> among the Romans, and among the Greeks, those derived from the **Eleusinian mysteries** <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone.</note> and the **Orgia** <note>original: *Оргіи* — In the ancient world, this term referred to secret rites or worship, particularly those of Dionysus, and did not carry the modern derogatory meaning.</note>. For although the Eleusinian mysteries, as well as the Orgia, contained important matters within them; nevertheless, it seems that those derived from them were not of the same character. Indeed, these were not as famous or respected in the ancient world as the former, where generally the mysteries related only to the pagan folk religion, or presented the history of the god to whom they were dedicated.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between ancient mysteries and philosophical schools. This page begins by completing a sentence about the lack of similarity between early philosophy and popular religion.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
Г 2

...were; there it is impossible to assume any similarity between philosophy and the **mysteries**; and it was only in very late times that philosophy took on a relationship with the popular religion.

The same applies to philosophy and the various schools, or parties, that arose within it. Some of them had no connection at all with the **sacraments** <note>original: *таинствами* — here used as a synonym for the secret religious mysteries.</note>. Although **Socrates** <note>The famous Athenian philosopher (c. 470–399 BC) who shifted the focus of philosophy toward ethics and human conduct.</note> wished to be initiated into the mysteries for the very reason that the obligation imposed in them might not hinder him from teaching the truth—from which one might quite probably guess that his teaching was fundamentally the same as what was taught in the mysteries—this, however, concerned only two doctrines: the **future state** <note>The condition of the soul after death.</note> and the existence of the Supreme Being. Furthermore, this great man banished from his philosophy everything that did not have a direct influence on the moral improvement of human beings; and for that reason, many things that constituted the subject of the mysteries fell away from his system. The same can also be said of the **Aristotelian**, **Stoic**, **Epicurean**, and many other schools. The entire question of whether the **mysteries** <note>original: *таинства* — referring to the secret religious rites of antiquity.</note> were identical to philosophy and its truths cannot be directly confirmed or denied, as will be quickly perceived by anyone who possesses some precise knowledge of both. ...with the **mysteries** <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world, often involving initiation and hidden knowledge.</note>, and that they can justly be considered authentic products of them. **Thales** the **Milesian** <note>Thales of Miletus (c. 624–545 BC), often hailed as the first philosopher in the Western tradition.</note> was taught by the priests in Egypt and brought their metaphysics and physics to the Greeks (*). His doctrines concerning the gods, and the origin and nature of things, are fundamentally the same as those taught among the Egyptians (**), even though little of his work remains to us. If, on the contrary, someone should say that **Thales** was taught Egyptian philosophy by the priests in Egypt, and not the mysteries, it is well known that all learning in Egypt was the exclusive domain of the priests. For them, instruction in the sciences could be regarded as nothing other than instruction in the mysteries.

This is seen even more clearly in the **Pythagorean school** <note>The school founded by Pythagoras, known for its secrecy, mathematical focus, and belief in the transmigration of souls.</note>, which adopted not only the teachings of the Egyptians but also their hidden, symbolic form—a subject I shall speak of specifically hereafter.

*Warburton* <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop and author of *The Divine Legation of Moses*, who argued that the ancient mysteries were designed by legislators to uphold morality and religion.</note> further states that the secret teachings of the mysteries could not have been metaphysical philosophical speculations about the divinity and the human soul; for in that case, the secret teachings of the philosophical schools would have been identical to the teachings of the mysteries, which he believes could not be so because of the difference in their subject matter. — I have already spoken about the difference in subject matter earlier. It is well known, and even *Warburton* agrees, that the secret teachings of the philosophical schools contained metaphysical speculations about God and the soul. Now the only question is: did the mysteries also engage in these speculations? And to this question, one can only answer in the affirmative. For in the mysteries, knowledge was not only received, but also imparted. Not only were prejudices and errors regarding the gods and what is told of them in mythological lore refuted; not only was it shown that concepts of the future state of the human soul according to poetic fables are irrational; but these popular **hieroglyphs** <term>hieroglyphs: Used here in a figurative sense to refer to myths, symbols, or allegories that hide a deeper, rational meaning from the uninitiated public.</term> were also explained, and more rational concepts were provided regarding the pri—

### V.
### On Certain Special Characteristics Pertaining to the Mysteries.

Before a more precise investigation of the ancient mysteries <note>The "mysteries" refers to the secret religious rites of the ancient Greek and Roman world, such as those at Eleusis.</note> themselves, I must also mention certain characteristics that were inherent to them and require more detailed examination. Among these is, first of all, the fact that they were celebrated at night. Although it cannot be proven with absolute certainty that this applied to all of them, it is highly probable due to the similarity found between various mysteries in so many respects, and because of their common origin.

Regarding the **Eleusinian** mysteries, it is known that they were celebrated during the night. **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), a Roman statesman and philosopher.</note>, in his explanation of a law prohibiting, among other things, nocturnal sacrifices and services, explicitly excludes the Eleusinian mysteries, which were likewise celebrated at night (*). When Emperor **Valentinian** <note>Valentinian I (reigned 364–375 CE), a Roman emperor who sought to restrict traditional pagan practices.</note> wished to forbid the Greeks from nocturnal sacrifices, Praetextatus <note>Vettius Agorius Praetextatus, a prominent Roman senator and priest who defended traditional religion.</note>, the Proconsul in Greece at that time, made representations to him—

### V.
### On Certain Special Features Pertaining to the Mysteries.

Before a more precise investigation of the ancient mysteries themselves, I must mention several features that were characteristic of them and which require a more detailed examination. To this belongs, first of all, the fact that they were celebrated at night. Although it cannot be proven with absolute certainty that this should be understood to apply to all of them, it is highly probable given the similarity found between various mysteries in so many respects, and because of their universal origin. Regarding the **Eleusinian** mysteries <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually in honor of Demeter and Persephone.</note>, it is well known that they were celebrated during the night time. **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), a Roman statesman and philosopher who was himself initiated into the Eleusinian mysteries.</note>, in his explanation of the law prohibiting, among other things, nocturnal sacrifices and services, explicitly excludes the Eleusinian mysteries, which were likewise celebrated at night (*). When the Emperor **Valentinian** <note>Valentinian I (reigned 364–375 CE), a Christian emperor who sought to curb pagan rituals.</note> intended to forbid the Greeks from performing nocturnal sacrifices, Pretextatus, then Proconsul in Greece, made a represen- <note>The word breaks off; it is the beginning of "представленіе" (representation or protest).</note>

...Tertullian <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155 – c. 220 CE) was a prolific early Christian author from Carthage who often wrote about contemporary pagan rituals.</note> reports regarding the mysteries of **Mithras** <note>The Mithraic mysteries were a Roman mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, practiced in underground temples called mithraea.</note> (*). Such a great similarity deserves a more precise investigation into the reasons for this custom. It is indisputable that the night was convenient for making the deepest impression upon the initiate during dramatic representations and for deceiving the sight with all manner of visions. ***Meiners*** presents this reason as the most natural and important one (**). In all mysteries, certain dramatic representations were made, which either concerned the history of the mysteries and the adventures of the deity under whose patronage they specifically fell, or in such a manner instilled

<page-num>60</page-num>

...and impressed certain truths upon the uneducated spectator. In the Middle Ages, it was likewise the custom to impress the truths of the Christian religion upon spectators through formal spectacles presented in churches. For a certain **Duke of Saxony** <note>This likely refers to Frederick the Peaceable (1273–1323), Landgrave of Thuringia. Legend states that after witnessing a play of the "Ten Virgins" in 1321, where the foolish virgins were denied mercy despite the intercession of Mary, he fell into a deep despair and eventually died from the shock.</note>, the performance of the **Ten Virgins** made such an impression that he fell ill and died after several days.

In the **mysteries** <term>mysteries: secret religious rituals of the ancient world (such as those at Eleusis) that were only open to initiated members</term>, however, not only were the history and adventures of the gods—under whose patronage they were held—dramatically represented; but even during the rites of **initiation** <term>initiation: the formal process of being admitted into a sacred or secret society</term> themselves, things occurred that produced a certain deception of the senses. Gods and deceased heroes appeared. They terrified the initiate with diverse images in order to test his steadfastness. They made sudden changes of light and darkness, and so forth. For all of this, machines <note>original: "машины" — refers to theatrical devices and stagecraft used to create special effects, similar to the "deus ex machina" of the Greek theater.</note> were required, which anyone could easily have observed in daylight, whereby the whole action would have lost much of its suddenness; on the contrary, at night, everything could be produced more conveniently and imperceptibly.

And thus, this is the true reason why the **sacraments** <term>sacraments: used here to mean 'sacred mysteries' or 'religious rites' (from the Latin *sacramenta*)</term> among the ancients were performed almost everywhere during the night. Thus says **Synesius**, Bishop of **Ptolemais** <note>Synesius (c. 373–414 AD) was a philosopher-bishop who often blended Neoplatonic thought with Christian doctrine.</note>: "*to the night we entrust the mysteries*." <note>original: "нощи ввѣряемъ мы таинства"</note> Although in Christian worship there were no deceptions produced by the aid of machines, nevertheless, in those times when Christians imitated pagan mysteries, the holiest acts of religion were concealed by the gloom of night. Indisputably, they wished to grant these actions a more venerable importance in this way, recognizing that nocturnal silence and darkness naturally produce a certain sacred shuddering in the human heart.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "law of silence" in the early Church. The sacred and venerable importance which they sought to impart to the <term>mysteries</term> in this manner was, without doubt, an important cause of this unshakeable silence, but not the only one. Looking closely at the so-called "great <term>mysteries</term>" <note>original: "таинства"; refers here to the higher grades of initiation in ancient Greek or Roman religions.</note>, we find a much more significant reason for it. They were, in the literal sense, the opposites of the prevailing folk religion <note>original: "народной религіи"; the official state-sanctioned pagan worship of the time.</note>. In them, they not only revealed universal error but also taught the truth, as I shall demonstrate hereafter. However, the folk religion was so closely and firmly bound to the entire social structure that it was impossible to overthrow it and openly present the truth in opposition to it without simultaneously affecting the social fabric in its various parts.

Another reason is also presented by the internal nature of the <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: "мистерій"; secret rites or doctrines.</note>. Although their founders were not philosophers, as Clement of Alexandria <note>A prominent Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who sought to bridge the gap between Greek philosophy and faith.</note> asserts, it is nevertheless known that the mysteries contained not only the opposites of the popular religion, but also much pertaining to the sciences from other branches of learning. In the ancient world, people thought differently than they do today. Knowledge was not shared with everyone. It was thought that communicating the sciences to the common people would debase them and, in a way, violate their sanctity. The Greeks, in my opinion, were the first to make the sciences common knowledge. I do not wish to re-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of scientific and religious secrecy. It completes the sentence from the previous page ("I do not wish to re-solve") and examines the Egyptian influence on secret knowledge, the Greek legal enforcement of silence, and the theories of Christoph Meiners.</meta>

...solve how extensive, or how limited, the learning of the Egyptians was; however, it is known that they kept their knowledge in secret. It is likely that those from whom they received it acted in the same way. To this reverence for the sciences—or, so to speak, this "jealousy"—we should probably largely attribute the profound silence observed regarding everything offered in the <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: "мистеріяхъ"; secret religious rites and doctrines of the ancient world.</note>. One might think that this motive would no longer apply to the Greeks, who did not hide their sciences in the slightest. Yet they passed on the mysteries to others in the same manner as they themselves had received them. This is clearly seen in the fact that it was not considered a crime for an uninitiated person to speak of things that were actually taught in the mysteries; conversely, an <term>initiate</term> <note>original: "посвященной"; someone who has been formally admitted into the secret rites.</note> was liable to the death penalty if they spoke of such things.

I cannot fail to also mention the thoughts of Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810) was a German philosopher and historian who wrote extensively on the history of religions.</note> regarding the reasons for this silence. He believes that the founders of the mysteries intended thereby to prevent all "free talk," all public investigations and judgments concerning this important aspect of religion. After their <term>initiation</term> <note>original: "посвященія"</note>, the initiates were free to think whatever they wished, but they dared not speak of it.

According to this view, the vow of silence was merely a lock that prevented the discovery of <term>legerdemain</term> <note>original: "ташеншпилерства"; derived from the German *Taschenspielerkunst*, referring to sleight-of-hand tricks or conjuring.</note>; much like how a certain Dutchman extracted a promise of silence from all those who wished to see his great **Wonder-Animal** <note>original: "Vonder-Deer", likely a phonetic Russian rendering of the Dutch *Wonder-Dier* or German *Wunder-Tier*. This refers to a common satirical anecdote about a hoax where an animal is simply turned backward in its stall.</note>, which had its head where other animals have a tail. If the mysteries consisted of nothing more than dramatic performances, then this reason might suffice; although it is not understood how they were maintained for so long, and how, in the many centuries through which they endured, no one was found who felt enough compassion for their fellow citizens to expose the deceptions, regardless of a vow of silence that was unjustly demanded. But it is even clearer when one considers how **Cicero**, **Plutarch**, and so many other great men of antiquity submitted to this law and always recalled it, speaking not of such "games," but of the truths and fundamental principles they had learned in the mysteries. At the very least, this reasoning does not apply to the Great Mysteries at all; and there had to be

<meta>catchword: different</meta>

<summary>The author disputes the theory that the ancient mysteries were mere hoaxes or magic tricks, arguing that the respect shown by figures like Cicero and Plutarch proves they contained genuine philosophical truths.</summary>
<keywords>mysteries, secrecy, Cicero, Plutarch, legerdemain, ancient philosophy, Great Mysteries</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the reasons for the secrecy surrounding ancient religious mysteries. It concludes a point regarding the motives for silence, transitioning from the idea of "legerdemain" to more profound philosophical and ritualistic justifications supported by historical scholars.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>

->Д 4<-

->VI.<-

...various reasons, which gave cause for such silence. It appears that *Meiners* <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German historian and philosopher known for his works on the history of religions.</note> in the following of his discourse perceived this himself; for he refers to the passage of *Varro* <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a prolific Roman scholar.</note> from *Augustine* <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, who often quoted Varro in "The City of God."</note> already cited by *Warburton* <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop and author of "The Divine Legation of Moses," which contains a famous analysis of the ancient mysteries.</note> (*).

The primary reasons, besides the fact that the ancients speak of them, are further confirmed by the very presentation of the teaching. It was not only metaphorical, but everything written about it was in <term>Hieroglyphs</term> <note>original: "Іероглифахъ"; here referring to sacred, symbolic characters used to hide meaning from the uninitiated.</note>, or in a secret script. The sacred writings among the Greeks, just as among the <term>Egyptians</term> <note>original: "Египтянъ"; the author links Greek mystery traditions to an older Egyptian source.</note>, were very conscientiously hidden from outsiders, and in the <term>Arcadian</term> <note>original: "Аркадскихъ"; referring to Arcadia, a region of the Peloponnese where ancient and primitive rites were preserved.</note> mysteries, they were kept within folding stones <note>original: "складныхъ камняхъ"; likely a reference to the "petroma," two fitted stone tablets between which sacred documents were kept during the Greater Mysteries.</note>. What was the purpose of all this? The reasons cited above were undoubtedly the most significant among those that prompted the covering of everything said and done in the <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: "мистеріяхъ"; secret religious rites and wisdom traditions of the ancient world.</note> with the deepest silence.

It cites scholarly authorities such as Meiners, Warburton, and Varro to support the gravity of this silence.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from a general discussion of ancient secrecy and symbolic language to a specific examination of the Eleusinian Mysteries. <page-num>72</page-num>

# VI.
## *On the Lesser Eleusinian Mysteries.* <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were divided into two parts: the "Lesser" mysteries, held in the spring as a preparation, and the "Greater" mysteries, held in the autumn.</note>

Of all the other mysteries of the ancients, not as much information has reached posterity as concerning the Eleusinian ones. Scarcely are some symbols of the so-called <term>Orphic mysteries</term> <note>original: "Орфическихъ таинствъ"; religious rites associated with the mythical poet Orpheus, focusing on the soul's journey and reincarnation.</note> known. From the Egyptian mysteries, specific fragments are known in places, quite insufficient for composing a whole from them. From the so-called *mysteries of Mithras* <note>original: "таинствъ Митры"; a Roman mystery religion centered on the deity Mithras, popular among the military.</note>, likewise, only disconnected discoveries have reached posterity, having no connection between them.

We have the most detailed accounts concerning the mysteries celebrated in the Greek city of Eleusis. It does not follow from this that they were the most important; however, they were visited more than others. **Cicero** says that peoples from the most distant countries of the world were initiated into them. For this reason, they in some way absorbed all other mysteries existing at that time in Greece, or at least eclipsed their fame. By having information about them, one can obtain quite accurate ideas about other mysteries of the ancient world. **Meursius**, **Warburton**, and **Meiners** published the best accounts of them. <note>**Johannes Meursius** (1579–1639) was a Dutch classical scholar; **William Warburton** and **Christoph Meiners** were previously cited historians of religion.</note>

<vocab>Eleusinian mysteries, Orphic mysteries, Egyptian mysteries, mysteries of Mithras, Cicero, Meursius, Warburton, Meiners</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the Eleusinian Mysteries as the primary case study for ancient secret rites, noting that while they may not have been the "most important," their immense popularity and the surviving scholarship make them the best window into the ancient mystery traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Eleusinian Mysteries, Orphic Mysteries, Mithraism, Egyptian mysteries, Cicero, historiography of religion, initiation, Meursius, Warburton, Meiners</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 72 regarding the scholarship on the Eleusinian Mysteries, specifically addressing the scholarly confusion caused by the division of the rites into two distinct parts.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>

accounts. However, a connoisseur and diligent critic will find that in some places these accounts require supplementation.

The mysteries celebrated in **Eleusis** <note>original: "Елевзисѣ"; an ancient Greek city famous as the site of the Eleusinian Mysteries, the most sacred of all ancient Greek religious rites.</note> were divided into the **great** and the **small**. This caused many difficulties for all the authors who wrote on this matter. For as the ancients, when speaking of them, did not always distinguish one from the other, things pertaining to the small mysteries are often attributed to the great mysteries, and vice versa.

This division arose from the very nature of things. For the *great* mysteries contained within them such matters that it was absolutely impossible to admit everyone to participate in them; partly because not all men were capable of understanding them; and partly because the religion established in <term>civil society</term> <note>original: "гражданскомъ обществѣ"; here referring to the official state religion and public laws of the Greek city-states.</note>, being tied to its very institution, could not be reconciled with them. On the contrary, the *small* mysteries contained within them such things that everyone not only could, but in some way was obliged to know, as will be more clearly seen from what follows.

In the proper sense, one could only say of the great mysteries that they were true <term>sacraments</term> <note>original: "таинства"; can be translated as "mysteries," "secrets," or "sacraments" depending on the theological context.</note>. For those mysteries which every citizen must know,

<summary>This page explains the fundamental division between the Greater and Lesser Eleusinian Mysteries, noting that the Greater rites were kept secret because they potentially conflicted with established state religion.</summary>

<keywords>Eleusinian Mysteries, Great Mysteries, Small Mysteries, civil society, sacraments, religious hierarchy, Greek religion</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 73, which suggested that if a "mystery" is something every citizen must know, the term itself seems contradictory. It transitions from the definition of the term to the specific preparatory rituals of the Lesser Mysteries.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

are a contradiction, and only those things can properly be called **mysteries** <note>original: "таинствами" (tainstvami); the author is playing on the dual meaning of the word as both a "secret" and a religious "sacrament."</note> which are kept from general knowledge. But do we not even today call certain Christian matters "mysteries of religion" which every Christian is required to know? Thus, the **Lesser Mysteries** may always bear this name; for silence concerning them was mandatory before all the uninitiated, and the uninitiated were not even permitted to enter the places to which the initiated had access.

The actual initiation into the Lesser Mysteries was preceded by certain rites that should be regarded as true preparations for them. These had to be undertaken by anyone wishing to gain closer access to the mysteries. There can be no doubt that these **preparatory exercises** were intended to further exalt the mysteries and instill greater reverence for them among the people. While we cannot say with absolute certainty what these preparations consisted of, it is known that they involved purifications or **ablutions**. The initiates were led to the river **Ilissus** <note>original: "Илиссъ"; a river in Athens, on the banks of which the Lesser Mysteries were celebrated in the month of Anthesterion.</note> and there doused with water. During this, they stood upon sanctified skins <note>Likely a reference to the *Dios Kodion* or the "Fleece of Zeus," a ram's skin used in Athenian purification rituals to absorb ritual impurity.</note>. The reason for this latter circumstance was undoubtedly so that they—

Although this expression is otherwise applied only to the **Great Mysteries**, and for that reason **Dionysius the Areopagite** divides the mysteries into three classes, namely: into <term>Καθαρσις</term> <note>original: *Katharsis*</note>, **purifications**; <term>Μυησις</term> <note>original: *Myesis*</note>, ***mystery***, and <term>Τελειωσις</term> <note>original: *Teleiosis*</note>, ***perfection*** (**); however, Theon’s designation is not incorrect in itself; for these preliminary purifications figuratively represented a **mystical death**. —are a contradiction; properly speaking, only those things of which not everyone can gain knowledge can be called <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: "таинствами"; in Russian, this word refers both to the ancient Greek mysteries and the Christian sacraments.</note>. Actual initiation into the <term>lesser mysteries</term> was preceded by certain rites, which should truly be regarded as preparations for them, and which had to be undertaken by all who wished to obtain closer access to the <term>mysteries</term>.
