After that, a sacrifice was offered. During initiation into the **Eleusinian Mysteries**, pregnant sows were sacrificed. This ritual pertained specifically to the deity under whose patronage these mysteries were held <note>The goddess Demeter, for whom the pig was a sacred animal representing fertility and the earth</note>. In general, pregnant sows were the customary sacrifice to this goddess. During the preliminary initiation into the **Orgia** <note>secret ritual rites, often associated with Dionysus or the Great Mother</note>, different sacrifices were undoubtedly employed. **Apuleius** <note>a Roman writer, c. 124–170 AD</note>, in the narrative of his own initiation, also mentions a sacrifice. Although he does not explicitly state what kind it was, it is understood from the entire structure of pagan worship that it was the same as that which was otherwise usually offered to **Isis**.

Besides sacrifices, those wishing to be initiated were required to prepare themselves through prayer, fasting, and abstinence. Certain foods, such as fish, beans, and apples, were strictly forbidden to them; they could not even approach these plants or touch them without being defiled. The prohibition of apples apparently stems from the story of **Proserpina** <note>also known as Persephone; she was bound to the underworld after eating seeds of a pomegranate, which was often referred to in older texts generically as an "apple"</note>; while the prohibition of fish and beans is undoubtedly of **Egyptian origin** <note>Pythagorean and Egyptian priestly traditions famously avoided beans and certain fish due to beliefs about reincarnation or ritual purity</note>. From the response that the initiates gave upon entering the temple—serving as a password—it is observed that during their preliminary initiation, they were given food and drink from the sacred...

Everything visible within the temple itself may have been connected to the initiation, the trials, and the purifications. A passage in Lucian’s *Cataplus* <note>original: *Kataplous* ("The Downward Journey" or "The Tyrant"); a satirical dialogue by Lucian of Samosata about souls being ferried to the underworld</note> makes this highly probable. It is as follows:

*Micyllus <note>A humble cobbler and the protagonist of the dialogue</note>. My God! What darkness! Where is the beautiful Megillus? Or how can one distinguish whether Phryne <note>A famous and famously beautiful Athenian courtesan</note> is more beautiful than Simmichus? Everything here is equal to one another and of the same color, and it is impossible to distinguish what is beautiful and what is more beautiful. My old worn-out cloak, which previously seemed poor, is now the same as royal purple. Both cannot be distinguished, and both are covered by the same gloom. But where are you, Cyniscus?*

Cyniscus <note>A Cynic philosopher who accompanies Micyllus to Hades</note>. *Here, I tell you, here, Micyllus! Let us go together, if you wish.*

Micyllus. *Very well; give me your hand. You are initiated into the Eleusinian mysteries, so tell me, how does it seem to you—does this state not resemble them?*

Cyniscus. *True. Look, someone comes with torches in their hands, casting terrifying and fearsome glances.*

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Eleusinian initiation rites, transitioning from the sensory darkness described in the previous passage from Lucian to the specific mythological dramas—the abduction of Persephone and the wanderings of Demeter—that formed the core of the Lesser Mysteries.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

### Was it a ***Fury*** ( * )?

Although everything that occurred is not fully known from this passage, one can nevertheless see that certain strange rites took place after the initial dedication itself. This is also confirmed by the aforementioned story of the two young ***Acarnanians*** <note>The author refers to a famous historical incident where two youths from Acarnania accidentally entered the temple of Demeter during the Mysteries; because they had not been initiated, their ignorance of the protocols betrayed them, and they were put to death for the sacrilege</note>, who, through their strange questions about what they had seen and felt within the temple, revealed that they had not been initiated.

But what exactly did they learn after all the initiations? I believe I am not mistaken in saying here that what we know of it consists only of ***fragments***. However, it is known that in the Lesser Eleusinian Mysteries <note>The preparatory stage of the initiation, held annually in the spring at Agrae, near Athens</note>, the story of the abduction of ***Proserpina*** <note>The Roman name for Persephone, daughter of Demeter</note> was dramatically represented, along with how ***Ceres*** <note>The Roman name for Demeter, the goddess of agriculture</note> searched for her daughter. According to the reports of the Church Fathers, and especially Clement of Alexandria <note>Titus Flavius Clemens, a Christian theologian who detailed pagan rites to argue against them</note>, the entire story of ***Ceres*** was presented; namely, how ***Jupiter*** <note>The Roman name for Zeus</note> compelled her by force to his will; the punishment he feigned to inflict upon himself to appease the goddess's wrath; the birth of ***Proserpina***, and how she was violated by her own father in the form of a serpent; the abduction of ***Proserpina*** by ***Pluto*** <note>The Roman name for Hades, ruler of the underworld</note>, and how ***Ceres*** [without]—

<summary>This page discusses the secretive nature of the Eleusinian Mysteries, suggesting that while the full details remain fragmented, the Lesser Mysteries centered on the dramatic reenactment of the myth of Demeter (Ceres) and Persephone (Proserpina).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the myth of Ceres (Demeter) and Proserpina (Persephone), comparing the dramatic nature of the Eleusinian Mysteries to other ancient mystery cults to demonstrate that these rites were essentially theatrical reenactments of divine tragedies.</meta>

...disconsolately searched for her daughter, on which occasion she shared the mysteries with the Athenians. Based on many indecent occurrences <note>The author likely refers to the "shameful" or ribald myths often criticized by early Christian writers, such as the story of Baubo exposing herself to make the grieving Demeter laugh</note> in this story, one might be tempted to think that this report is groundless. But comparing the Eleusinian mysteries with others, it is impossible to consider it a fiction; for in all other mysteries, the story of the deity under whose patronage they were held was dramatically represented. In the *Orgies* <note>Specifically, the Dionysian Mysteries</note>, the entire story of *Bacchus* was presented. In the *Phoenician mysteries*, the entire story of *Adonis* was represented. In the *Mysteries of Venus*, the story of this goddess, the transformation of *Saturn*, and everything belonging thereto, were depicted in a formal drama. In the mysteries of *Isis*, which had already been made *Greek* <note>Refers to the Hellenized version of the Egyptian cult that spread throughout the Greco-Roman world</note>, the slaying of *Osiris* by *Typhon* was shown, and the gathering of the mournful remains of his body by *Isis*. For this reason, *Clement of Alexandria* says that in the mysteries they imitated nothing other than the slayings and burials of the gods. With this agrees also what *Cicero* says: *Only ask what kind of tombs are shown in Greece, and once initiated, remember what is said in the mysteries. Then you will see how far this ex-* <note>original: "Quaere, quorum demonstrentur sepulcra in Graecia; reminiscere, quoniam es initiatus, quae tradantur mysteriis; tum denique quam hoc late pateat intelleges." Cicero is arguing in his 'Tusculan Disputations' that the gods were originally mortal men whose deaths were recorded in their home lands</note>

Following these historical representations were the so-called dogmatic ones, which contained the teaching on the future state of souls departing from the body. It is probable that they began with the <term>torments of Tartarus</term> <note>In Greek mythology, the deep abyss used as a dungeon of torment for the wicked</note>, and the initiates were presented with, in a certain manner, the entire gloomy realm of shadows. The pagans accepted a middle state, in which those were purified who, although they had not committed crimes deserving of hellish punishment, were nonetheless not so pure as to participate in the joys of <term>Elysium</term> <note>The Elysian Fields; the final resting place of the souls of the heroic and the virtuous in Greek mythology</note>. The most delightful fields were visible, with happy souls walking upon them, and how they received the reward for their virtues in various states of bliss. Exquisite music was heard, the most lovely voices; and just as much art as had previously been employed to produce fear and horror in the soul, just as much was then applied to fill it with the greatest joy. —sented, in part, the torments of *Tantalus* <note>A figure in Greek mythology punished in the afterlife by being made to stand in a pool of water beneath a fruit tree, with both eternally receding from his grasp</note>, *Sisyphus* <note>Condemned to push a boulder up a hill for eternity, only for it to roll back down each time</note>, and *Ixion* <note>Punished by being bound to a forever-spinning fiery wheel</note>, and in part, the blessed souls at the tables of the gods; then to what purpose did all these arrangements serve? In that case, the mysteries would indeed have contained nothing that was not already known to anyone who had read a few poets; no one would have had any need for an initiation that provided him with nothing better than what he had already learned long ago. The direct opposite of this is found in the fact that in the Lesser Mysteries, they dramatically represented the history and deeds of the gods under whose patronage they were held. circumstances. Undeniably, these garments were of low cost, and likely did not differ from ordinary ones; for some wore them until even a single rag remained. In all probability, this was the origin of the white garments worn in former times by the newly baptized <note>Russian: новокрещенными - those recently receiving the Christian sacrament of baptism</note> after their baptism; for there is not the slightest trace of them before the mysterious teaching <note>The author implies a connection between the pagan "mysteries" and early Christian ritual practices</note> was introduced among Christians.

Such were the lesser **Eleusinian mysteries**. Although it is not subject to any doubt that other mysteries, according to the variety of gods to whom they were dedicated, differed from them in many parts; this concerned only the dramatic representations of the deity’s various adventures, as well as the sacrifices, games, and choirs. In essence, however, they all resembled one another; and this essence was the teaching on the immortality of the soul, and on the punishments and rewards in the future life. Although ***Meiners*** <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian known for his work on the history of religions</note> thinks that, besides the Eleusinian mysteries, the immortality of the soul was taught only in the **Bacchic Orgies** <note>The ecstatic rituals associated with Bacchus (Dionysus), the god of wine and theater</note>, ***Porphyry*** <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234 – c. 305 AD), a Neoplatonic philosopher who wrote extensively on ancient religion</note> says this also about the mysteries of **Mithras** <note>The Mithraic mysteries were a Roman mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, popular among the military</note>. From what is narrated by ***Apuleius*** about his initiation, it is evident that that

<meta>catchword: же (which/same)</meta>

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: Eleusinian mysteries, Meiners, Bacchic Orgies, Porphyry, Mithras, Apuleius, immortality of the soul, newly baptized.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the humble nature of initiates' clothing, linking it to early Christian baptismal robes, and argues that the core of all ancient mystery religions was the doctrine of the immortality of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Eleusinian Mysteries, immortality of the soul, baptismal garments, Mithras, Bacchic Orgies, Porphyry, afterlife rewards and punishments</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the commonalities between various ancient mystery cults, specifically noting that the core teachings on the soul and the afterlife remained consistent regardless of the deity. It transitions into a new section regarding the "Great Mysteries."</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

...was also the subject in the mysteries of **Isis** <note>The Egyptian goddess whose cult became one of the most popular mystery religions in the Greco-Roman world</note>. Therefore, one can conclude with great probability that they all resembled one another in this respect, although they differed in other parts.

The question is also raised: were moral precepts given on this occasion? Dramatic representations were themselves, in a way, an instruction adapted to the sensibilities of the common man, and thus all the more piercing. It is also true that almost everything was expressed through outward appearances and rituals; this is why even the most virtuous man, without initiation <note>The formal process of being admitted into the secret knowledge of a religious cult</note>, was not promised a happy lot in the future life. However, it would be a contradiction in itself if they taught of future punishments and rewards without simultaneously showing the ways to avoid the former and to become a partaker of the latter.

---

### VII.
#### Concerning the Great Mysteries.

->Not all at once, says **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher; this quote likely refers to his "Natural Questions," where he discusses how the secrets of the universe, like the mysteries, are not revealed to everyone at once</note>, are certain sacred things communicated. **Eleusis** <note>The town in Greece where the most famous of all secret religious rites, the Eleusinian Mysteries, were performed annually</note> conceals something that is revealed only to those who return to the mysteries<-

<summary>This page argues that all ancient mystery cults shared a fundamental focus on moral instruction and the afterlife, expressed through sensory drama. It introduces the "Great Mysteries," quoting Seneca to explain that their deepest secrets were only revealed gradually over time.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, mysteries, Eleusis, Seneca, moral precepts, initiation, dramatic representations, afterlife, Great Mysteries</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the gradual nature of initiation into the mysteries, completing the thought from the previous page regarding how nature and sacred things are revealed slowly to the seeker.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

...approach <note>Continuing from the previous page: "only to those who return to the mysteries, they approach."</note>. And nature does not all at once reveal her sanctuaries. We already consider ourselves initiated, yet we are still in the vestibule <note>The entrance or porch of a temple; the author uses this to describe the state of being only at the beginning of true understanding</note>. These mysteries are not opened to everyone without distinction. They are preserved and enclosed in the innermost sanctuary. For this reason, **Clement of Alexandria** <note>A prominent Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who wrote extensively about Greek mystery cults, often comparing them to Christian teachings</note> quite rightly considers the "Lesser Mysteries" as preparations and preliminary exercises for the "Greater" ones.

These Great Mysteries, in the proper sense, were the internal essence of pagan religion; what was told to the initiates therein was of such a nature that, if it were declared to everyone, it would have produced a significant change in outward religion and in civil society itself. They contained within themselves that knowledge which, for various reasons, was hidden from all the uninitiated, and was revealed only to a very few—namely, to the most select part of the priests and to the rulers. For this reason, the following names properly belong to them: *full vision* <note>original Greek: *ἐποψία* (epopsia); the final stage of initiation in the Eleusinian Mysteries</note>, *ineffable things* <note>original Greek: *ἀπόῤῥητα* (aporrhēta); secret matters that are forbidden to be spoken of in public</note>, *perfection* <note>original Greek: *τελετή* (teletē); a rite of initiation or a religious festival</note>, and so forth. This was the authentic part of the mysteries concerning the sciences <note>In the 18th-century context, "sciences" often refers to higher knowledge, philosophy, or the study of the divine</note>; all else were merely representations, hero- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the next page begins with "glyphs," referring to Hieroglyphs</note>

<summary>This page defines the "Great Mysteries" as the secret, inner core of ancient religion, intended only for a select elite because of their potential to disrupt social and religious order. It distinguishes these high rites from the "Lesser Mysteries" using classical Greek terminology for initiation.</summary>

<keywords>Great Mysteries, Lesser Mysteries, Clement of Alexandria, Epopsia, Aporrhēta, Teletē, initiation rites, secret knowledge, pagan religion</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the distinction between the "Lesser" and "Greater" Mysteries, moving from the symbolic preparations to the direct revelation of truth and the rigorous initiation process required for the higher stages.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Institution</meta>

...glyphs and sensory representations <note>Visible symbols or allegorical signs used to represent spiritual concepts to those not yet fully enlightened</note>; but in the Greater Mystery, the veil that hid the truth was removed, and the initiates had the happiness of beholding the truth without any covering. In the Lesser Mysteries, rituals were the primary focus; in contrast, in the Great Mysteries, these were merely secondary matters, and the main objective consisted of the instruction received.

The first matter subject to investigation here concerns initiation into these Great Mysteries, as well as the rituals and trials through which they were imparted. It is highly probable that all of this was practiced, and it seems unlikely that initiation into the Lesser Mysteries was deemed sufficient on its own. Nor is it likely—as **Meiners** <term>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian known for his works on the history of religions and the "mysteries" of antiquity</term> suggests—that they simply relied on the fact that all who approached the Great Mysteries were noble men of known character. Since even the Lesser Mysteries were preceded by such preparations, one should expect this even more for those mysteries that were far more significant. However, what can be inferred here with probability is also known in itself. **Cicero** <term>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman orator and statesman, who was himself initiated into the Eleusinian Mysteries and wrote of their civilizing influence</term>, when speaking of those things offered only in the Great Mysteries, speaks simultaneously of the initiations into them.

<summary>This page distinguishes the Lesser Mysteries as ritual-based and the Greater Mysteries as instruction-based, arguing that the latter required rigorous trials and formal initiation despite some scholarly claims to the contrary.</summary>
<keywords>Greater Mysteries, Lesser Mysteries, initiation, Christoph Meiners, Cicero, ritual vs. instruction, revelation of truth</keywords>

<page-num>96</page-num>

The institution established by ***Pythagoras*** <term>Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BC), the Greek philosopher whose school was structured as a secret brotherhood with rigorous rules of silence and levels of initiation</term> regarding his inner disciples, which was nothing other than a faithful imitation of the Egyptian mysteries, also serves as confirmation of this, as do the trials that he himself had to undergo in order to participate in their inner instruction. The formal confession required from ***Lysander*** <note>This likely refers to an anecdote where the Spartan general Lysander was asked to confess his misdeeds before initiation into the Samothracian mysteries</note> during his initiation into the mysteries—not at the lesser, but rather at the great mysteries—was necessarily assumed. When ***Proclus*** <term>Proclus Lycaeus (412–485 AD), a late Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the spiritual and symbolic meaning of the ancient mysteries</term> speaks not only of the lesser but specifically of the great mysteries, saying that they raised the soul from a death-like life to the divine (*); then it is easily perceived that he speaks not only of the discovery of certain truths, but also of purifications and preparations. ***Gregory of Nazianzus*** <term>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), a prominent Christian theologian who often used the terminology of pagan "mysteries" to explain the progression of Christian faith and baptism</term> says that no one received access to them without first undergoing certain trials and purifications, of which he names twelve degrees. But this is all that we know of it; from which it is evident that regarding these rites, an equally inviolable obser-

vance of silence was maintained, just as it was in the consideration of the things themselves. Perhaps by diligent concealment of the manner of initiation, they wished to compensate for the impossibility of hiding all the inner truths of the Great Mysteries from the sight of outsiders, so that nothing about them could be known.

Now the question is asked: *What then was contained within this hidden interior of the pagan religion, or the so-called Great Mysteries?* 

The ancients always regarded the final rites ( <term>teletai</term> <note>original: τελετάς; a Greek term meaning "perfections" or "completions," used to describe the highest initiatory ceremonies</note> ) as the repository of all the highest knowledge. ***Clement of Alexandria*** <term>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150–215 AD), a Christian theologian who, despite his criticism of paganism, often used the language of the mysteries to describe the path to Christian knowledge</term> , who otherwise speaks so contemptuously of the mysteries, says of them: *The Great Mysteries concern all things in general. Nothing is left for further study; instead, one learns to perceive nature and its works.* In this same vein, the author of the ***Great Etymologist*** ( <term>Etymologicum Magnum</term> <note>A vast Byzantine encyclopedia of the Greek language compiled around 1150 AD, which preserved many fragments of ancient religious thought</note> ) says that within them, accurate concepts of the gods were obtained. 

If we wish to form an idea of what was taught in the Great Mysteries based on both of these important accounts, then the knowledge to which the initiates were led was partly theological and partly physical <note>In this context, "physical" refers to "physiologia" or the study of the natural world and the laws of the universe</note> in nature. Such a connection is not surprising; for in philoso-

...in the schools of the ancients, both were very closely bound to one another. In this regard, I cannot omit the following observation: everything we know about the **Great Mysteries** <term>Great Mysteries</term> <note>The highest level of ancient initiation ceremonies, most famously the Eleusinian Mysteries, which promised secret knowledge of the divine and the afterlife.</note> of the ancients concerns only the religious knowledge revealed in them; whereas, concerning the other parts, we know almost nothing. 

Why is this the case? Were the other subjects less significant? I suspect, rather, that they required a seal of silence even stricter than the others, and I find no explanation for this anywhere except in the rise of **Christianity**. After the advent of Christianity, the previously hidden teachings were revealed partly by the Christians themselves, and partly by the **pagans** <note>original: "язычники"; here referring to the practitioners of the old Greco-Roman religions.</note>. The latter did so to defend their religion from the shameful reproaches it—not without reason—received from the Christians, striving to demonstrate that their faith was not as empty or contrary to reason as it appeared externally.

Firstly, the errors of the common folk religion were revealed within the Great Mysteries. It was shown that the external religion, to which the people clung, was not the true one. The **Mystagogue** <term>Mystagogue</term> <note>original: "Мистагогъ"; a priest or teacher who initiates others into secret religious rites.</note> or **Hierophant** <term>Hierophant</term> <note>original: "Іерофантъ"; the high priest of the mysteries who "revealed the sacred objects" to the initiates.</note> demonstrated that this entire figurative religion was merely a kind of veil,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Mysteries," specifically how the "Mystagogue" or "Hierophant" (the high priest of the mysteries) would reveal the hidden truths behind the public religious myths. ...veil, or a shroud, beneath which the true religion was hidden; that Jupiter, Juno, Minerva, Mars, Venus, and Mercury, and the entire high retinue of gods honored by the ignorant and superstitious masses <note>original: "чернію" (the rabble or common folk)</note>, were not gods, but in fact only mortal men. It was revealed where they were born, where they lived, and where their funerary monuments could still be found; that certain services they rendered to the human race moved the people to honor their memory, and thus they eventually attained the status of being worshipped as gods. **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher who often wrote about the nature of the gods.</note> states this directly and clearly intimates that this was taught in the **Great Mysteries** <term>Great Mysteries</term>. Is not the whole of heaven, he asks, filled with human beings?

> *If I ventured to investigate ancient records, and among them especially those written by Greek authors, I would find that even the gods of the highest rank ascended to heaven from among us. One need only ask: whose graves are shown in Greece? If you have been initiated, just remember what is told of this in the mysteries; then you will see how far-reaching this is.* — <note>This passage is a translation of Cicero's *Tusculan Disputations* (Book I, Sections 12–13). This philosophical view—that gods were originally historical heroes—is known as **Euhemerism**.</note>

Although there is not a single nation that does not believe in the existence of gods, it is also known that very many...

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Mysteries" and the philosophical perspective on the origin of gods. It addresses the tension between the hidden "true religion" of the elite and the public "folk religion" used to maintain social order.</meta>

...[very many] have completely false notions about the gods (*). So then, did the **Mystagogues** <term>Mystagogues</term> <note>From the Greek *mystagogos*; these were the priests who led and initiated others into the sacred mysteries.</note>, who were themselves priests, portray themselves as deceivers who kept the common people in a state of delusion? — I do not think so, for this would do little honor to the priests themselves. Rather, they presented themselves as what, in a certain sense, they truly were: the guardians of the true religion. 

On the other hand, they presented the public folk religion for exactly what it was: a system that had arisen partly from the poets, partly from the lawmakers, and partly from the philosophers. It had been established in accordance with the laws of the state and the general character of the people, and it could not be discarded without causing a great upheaval. This is how **Scaevola** <note>Quintus Mucius Scaevola Pontifex (d. 82 BCE), a Roman jurist and high priest who categorized religion into three types: poetic, philosophical, and political.</note> explains it in the works of **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), a prolific Roman scholar whose lost works on religion are frequently cited by later Christian authors.</note>, who is cited by **Augustine** in his books *The City of God* (**). For this reason, Varro also suggests that there are many truths in religion which ought to be hidden from the people; and conversely, there are many errors in which the people should be left.

And so, in the **Great Mysteries**, first of all, the entire veil <note>original: "покровъ"</note> was removed...

...errors and superstitions from the eyes of the initiated, who were called **Epopts** <term>Epopts</term> <note>From the Greek *epoptes*, meaning "overseer" or "one who has seen." This was the highest grade of initiate in the Eleusinian Mysteries, who had witnessed the final revelation.</note>, because they had reached a state of free and unhindered contemplation of the truth. 

On this occasion, it was undoubtedly necessary for a great deal of ancient history to be explained; for it was then that they learned that the gods had been human beings, and they simultaneously received knowledge of the many various services these figures had rendered to the human race in former times, for which a grateful posterity had elevated them to such high dignity. For this reason, in the **Kabiric** <term>Kabiric mysteries</term> <note>The mysteries of the Cabiri (Kabeiroi) were ancient secret rites held on the islands of Samothrace and Lemnos, involving deities associated with fertility and protection at sea.</note> mysteries, formal genealogical records of these supposed gods were declared (*).

However just this may be, anyone will immediately notice that this explanation for the errors of folk religion was not common to all mysteries. Instead, it was found only among those nations who worshipped gods who had indeed previously been human. This was present in the Greek mysteries, but it was not found among the Egyptians—at least not in those times when the Egyptian religion had not yet lost its original character to the penetrating influence of Greek superstition. Egyptian deities were not deceased men, but rather...

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Egyptian mysteries differed from the Greek ones. While Greek mysteries revealed that gods were formerly mortal men, the author argues that true Egyptian religion was based on allegories of nature, not the deification of humans.</meta>

...persons representing objects of nature.

Although **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian who discussed pagan religions in his work *The City of God*.</note> cites a letter from **Alexander the Great** <note>King of Macedon and conqueror of Egypt. The "Letter to his Mother" mentioned here was a common reference in antiquity, though modern scholars often consider it a later rhetorical invention.</note>, in which he informs his mother that the Egyptian high priest **Leo** <note>Also known as Leon of Pella, a priest who allegedly explained the human origins of the gods to Alexander.</note> revealed to him concerning the gods—namely, that they were nothing other than mortal men—and **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a prolific Roman scholar and writer known for his vast erudition.</note> says in Augustine’s work that **Isis** and **Serapis** have a finger placed upon their lips to signify that one must remain silent about the fact that they were once human (*). But Varro—without wishing to offend his otherwise great erudition—was a poor connoisseur in this matter, and an even worse interpreter of the Egyptian religion. If Alexander’s letter is not a fiction, and Leo did indeed reveal the aforementioned to him, then this applies only to the **Greek** deities and those Egyptian ones modeled after the Greek example. For in uncorrupted **Egyptianism** <term>Egyptianism</term> <note>The author uses this term to refer to the authentic, original religious system of ancient Egypt before it was syncretized with or misinterpreted by Greek and Roman thinkers.</note>, there were no known gods who had previously been human beings. And so, if in the Egyptian mysteries they removed the veil of error from the initiate, they undoubtedly explained to him the entirety of **animal-worship** <term>animal-worship</term> <note>In Russian *skotosluzhenie* (literally "cattle-service"). This refers to zoolatry, the Egyptian practice of venerating animals as earthly manifestations of divine principles.</note>, together with its causes and significance; and **Osiris**, **Isis**, **Neith**, **Hathor**, **Ptah**, **Kneph**... <note>A list of primary Egyptian deities: Osiris (underworld/fertility), Isis (motherhood/magic), Neith (creation/weaving), Hathor (love/joy), Ptah (craftsmanship/creation), and Kneph (the breath of life/creator spirit).</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 103, where the author cited Cicero to argue that the ancient mysteries taught the "nature of things" rather than just the history of mortal men turned into gods. This page transitions into a lament for the lost knowledge of these secret rites.</meta>

It is a matter of regret that these insights and discoveries were lost along with the <term>mysteries</term> <note>secret religious rites of the ancient world</note> that contained them, and were thus hidden from the sight of posterity. For how many perspectives would have been opened through them into the ancient history of nations and heroes, and into their physics and <term>cosmogony</term> <note>theories regarding the origin of the universe</note>. Today, we can only say that in the mysteries, all of this was set forth. — But what was taught within them? — Over this, time and ruin have spread a veil; and who will be able to lift it once again?

The first step toward the knowledge of truth is always the recognition of error. This is evident in the ancient mysteries as well. First, they revealed the prevailing error of popular religion and removed the veil of <term>superstition</term> from the eyes of the <term>initiate</term> <note>one who has been formally admitted into the secret knowledge of the mysteries</note>; but this would not have been a complete knowledge if they had left him there. <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810) was a German philosopher and historian known for his works on the history of religions.</note> Meiners says quite rightly: *In the mysteries, they not only took away, but also gave; they not only destroyed the old building of errors, but also constructed a new, magnificent building of saving truths, of which all the ancients believed the common folk, due to their blindness of mind, were entirely incapable of understanding.* — In this latter reason, I do not agree with him; but I think,

<summary>The author laments the loss of the specific philosophical and scientific knowledge contained within ancient mysteries, noting that these rites aimed to replace popular superstition with a "magnificent building of saving truths" reserved for the initiated.</summary>
<keywords>ancient mysteries, cosmogony, Christoph Meiners, superstition, initiation, religious history, secret knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, where the author disagreed with the idea that the common people were mentally incapable of understanding higher truths. Instead, he argues that the secrecy of the mysteries was a matter of political and social necessity.</meta>

...all of this was kept hidden from the people, because external religion was already so closely intertwined with the civil order that it was impossible to shake the religious foundation without at the same time unsettling the entire state. Through this revelation—as if toppling idols from their thrones—they conversely revealed to the <term>initiates</term> <note>individuals who had been formally admitted to the secret knowledge of the mysteries</note> the one true God, the Creator and Ruler of all things. 

Accordingly, Chrysippus <note>a Greek Stoic philosopher of the 3rd century BCE</note> is quoted in the *Etymologicum Magnum* <note>a massive 12th-century Greek dictionary and encyclopedia</note> saying that in the mysteries, correct notions of the deity were obtained, and the soul was required to possess a certain strength to maintain silence regarding these most righteous concepts before the uninitiated. *The Unknown God* <note>original Greek: "Αγνωστος Θεός" (Agnostos Theos)</note>, to whom the Athenians built an altar, was perhaps none other than the Most High God who was revealed in the mysteries but remained unknown to the public; it may even be that the worship offered to Him at that time was derived from the mysteries themselves. Clement of Alexandria <note>an early Christian theologian (c. 150 – 215 AD) who sought to find common ground between Greek philosophy and Christian faith</note>, though otherwise a zealous opponent of the mysteries, admits this, and even believes that the word of truth was hidden within their sanctuaries just as it was among the Jews in the <term>Holy of Holies</term> <note>the most sacred inner sanctum of the Tabernacle and later the Temple in Jerusalem</note>.

<summary>The author explains that the secrets of the ancient mysteries were guarded to prevent political upheaval, as they revealed a single true God behind the popular polytheistic idols. He suggests the "Unknown God" of the Athenians was actually this secret deity, a view supported even by some early Christian critics.</summary>

<keywords>mysteries, monotheism, civil order, Chrysippus, Unknown God, Athens, Clement of Alexandria, Holy of Holies, religious secrecy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how ancient secret societies and religious "mysteries" preserved the concept of monotheism—the belief in one God—beneath the surface of public polytheism. The previous page explored how the "Unknown God" of the Athenians might have been the God of the mysteries. Catchword "Столѣ" likely refers to "Столѣтіе" (Century) or "Столѣ" (Table) on the following page.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

In the Samothracian mysteries <note>Secret religious rites performed on the island of Samothrace, centered on the "Great Gods" known as the Cabeiri</note>, this same teaching regarding the existence of a single supreme Being was also offered. Whatever opinion one might hold regarding the so-called **Orphic** poems <note>A collection of ancient Greek poems attributed to the legendary Orpheus, though often considered by scholars to be of later, Neopythagorean or Neoplatonic origin</note> cited periodically in the works of the ancients—even if every last one of them were regarded as fictional and forged—it is nonetheless certain that their authors recognized the doctrine of a single God as being that which was taught in the great mysteries. In one of these poems, the following is found:

*I wish to speak to the initiated; but first close the doors against the profane, who wander from the right path prescribed to all by the Divinity. Hearken, Musaeus <note>A legendary pre-Homeric poet and seer, often associated with the Orphic tradition</note>, son of the beautiful Selene <note>In Greek mythology, the personification of the Moon</note>! Now I freely utter the truth. Let not old prejudices deprive you of a blessed eternity. — Behold the single Ruler of the world, the Immortal One, just as the ancient teaching reveals Him to us. He is unique and exists of Himself alone. All things are produced by Him, and He Himself acts in all things; no mortal has ever seen Him, and He is perceived only by the reason <note>Original: "въ разумѣ" (v razume). This refers to the higher intellect or spiritual perception rather than mere logic</note>. There is no God besides Him (*).*

<page-num>107</page-num>

And so true and so exalted were the concepts regarding the one and supreme God taught to the initiates in the great mysteries, that I do not wish to deny entirely that polytheism <note>The belief in or worship of more than one god</note> occasionally became entangled with them in certain places. At the very least, this is evident in some of the **Orphic** hymns. Yet, who has not occasionally strayed onto such a path? Even the mystics of the Middle Ages were not immune to this, and thus it should not seem strange to anyone that we occasionally find similar diversions here—crossroads from which even those equipped with far better spiritual aids were unable to protect themselves.

It remains quite uncertain whether the method of instruction regarding God used in the mysteries was the same as that later employed by **Plato** and **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Citium (c. 334–262 BC), the founder of the Stoic school of philosophy</note>; namely, leading a person to contemplate the world and, from that observation, compelling them to deduce the existence of a Creator. It seems to me that this method is not yet proven by those passages in which **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator and philosopher</note> discusses the **Eleusinian** <note>Initiation ceremonies for the cult of Demeter and Persephone based at Eleusis</note> and **Samothracian mysteries**. He suggests that in these rites, one learned more about the "nature of things" <note>original: "натура вещей" (natura rerum) — a phrase referring to the physical laws and origins of the universe</note> than the specific nature of the gods. This is also suggested when **Clement of Alexandria** <note>A prominent Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who often wrote about the relationship between Greek philosophy and faith</note> states that within the mysteries, one learns to understand the whole of nature.

<summary>The author argues that while the ancient mysteries taught monotheism, they were sometimes influenced by polytheism. He also questions whether these secret societies used a "natural theology" approach—deducing God from nature—similar to later philosophers like Plato and Zeno.</summary>

In the <term>Samothracian mysteries</term> <note>religious rites held on the island of Samothrace dedicated to the "Great Gods," known for their focus on protection and the afterlife</note>, this teaching regarding the existence of a single supreme Being was also offered. Whatever opinion one might hold regarding the so-called **Orphic** poems <note>religious verses attributed to the mythical singer Orpheus</note> cited occasionally in the works of the ancients; even if all of them without exception were considered fictional and forged; it is nevertheless known that their authors recognized the teaching of the one God as that which was taught in the great mysteries. In one of these poems, the following is found: *I wish to speak to the initiates; but first, close the doors against the <term>profane</term> <note>the uninitiated or those outside the sacred circle</note>, those who stray from the right path prescribed to all by the Deity. There is no God besides Him* (*).

So true and so high were the concepts taught in the great mysteries to the initiates regarding the one and supreme God. I do not wish to entirely deny that polytheism <note>The belief in or worship of more than one god</note> occasionally intruded into them in certain places. It is quite uncertain whether the method of instruction concerning God in the mysteries was the same as that later employed by **Plato** and **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Citium, the founder of the Stoic school of philosophy</note>; namely, that they led a person to contemplate the world, and from that observation compelled him to draw conclusions about the **Creator**. and its works <note>The author is finishing a thought from the previous page regarding the "nature of things" (Latin: *natura rerum*), personified here as a feminine entity in the Russian grammar.</note>. In both of these places, it is not the method of instruction that is being discussed, but rather the fact that the teaching regarding the gods of popular religion was explained through nature, and that they were interpreted as personified objects of nature. A greater proof of this could have been the passage from **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus (129–216 AD), a Greek physician and philosopher who saw the complexity of the body as proof of divine design</note> cited by **Meiners** <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher known for his history of religions</note>. However, the aforementioned method of instruction is not yet proven by this, and that which was learned in the mysteries regarding the nature of things could only have been confirmations of a revealed truth, rather than the means of attaining knowledge of it. This also applies to the consideration of that teaching: it would indeed be a very strange kind of revelation if one were taught something that one could have arrived at on one's own.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether the ancient Egyptians possessed true knowledge of the Divine. This question must naturally seem strange to anyone who has read everything I have written thus far about the mysteries; indeed, I would not even have proposed it, had the knowledge of the **Egyptians** in our times not been contested by that very same learned man who wrote better than anyone else on the mysteries of the ancients. In the opinion of **Meiners** (*), the **Egyptian priests** were the same as the **Jugglers** <note>The author uses 'Jugglers' here in the 18th-century sense to describe the medicine men or spiritual leaders of Indigenous American peoples</note> in **America**, the **Shamans** in **Siberia**, the **Talapoins** <note>A term used by early European travelers to describe Buddhist monks in Southeast Asia, particularly Thailand</note> in **Siam**, the **Bonzes** <note>A term for Buddhist monks in East Asia, particularly China and Japan</note> in **China**, and so on; in short, they were sacred deceivers, respectable charlatans, and <term>sleight-of-hand artists <note>original: 'mashenshpilery,' from the German 'Taschenspieler,' meaning conjurers or street performers who use trickery</note></term>; and their mysteries were not teachings, nor knowledge pertaining to the sciences, but merely rituals and charlatanry. It seems that this learned man was led to these thoughts, firstly, by the similarity found between the **Egyptian priests** and **Jugglers**—namely, that with the former, just as with the latter, the priestly rank was hereditary and passed in an uninterrupted order from father to son; and furthermore, that not all classes of priests, but only a few among them, exclusively possessed the mysteries.

Undoubtedly, this perspective possesses a certain appearance of fairness; however, upon precise investigation, it proves insufficient. If we were to categorize as **Jugglers** and sacred <term>sleight-of-hand artists <note>original: 'tashenshpilery,' from the German 'Taschenspieler,' referring to those who perform deceptive magic or 'pocket-tricks' to fool an audience</note></term> all those priests whose office was hereditary, what then should we think of the **Jews**? With them, the priestly rank was likewise bound to a single sacred lineage, just as in **Egypt**, and the high-priestly dignity passed from father to son. Among the **Greeks** themselves, various roles within the mysteries were similarly hereditary, as I have already mentioned; yet **Meiners** makes an exception for the **Greeks**. Among them, only a few priests were admitted to the great mysteries; nevertheless, he regards their mysteries not as empty ceremonies and pious trickery, but as containing genuine scientific knowledge.

To show that the **Egyptians** possessed no scientific knowledge within their mysteries, **Meiners** offers the following arguments:

1) “If they had so much to conceal, why is it not possible to name and specifically define at least some of those branches of knowledge?”

I reply to this: concerning the **Greek mysteries**, only scattered words have likewise come down to us. But is it possible to draw such a conclusion: 

> “We cannot determine what branches of knowledge the **Egyptians** taught in their mysteries, and therefore they possessed none”?

> “The **Egyptians** possessed no knowledge relating to the sciences (in which teachers are indisputably presupposed); because they had not teachers, but only sacred **masters of ceremonies**?”

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the German scholar Christoph Meiners. The previous page ended by stating that Meiners actually admitted the Egyptians possessed secret knowledge that was not revealed to everyone...</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

...except after long-term trials; that these <term>mysteries</term> <note>Ancient religious rites, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were kept secret from the uninitiated and often involved complex rituals and teachings.</note> contained within themselves the ancient history of the state, observations from the natural history of their land, their teachings on the movements of celestial bodies, and their opinions on the origin of the world, as well as on the nature of the Deity (*). One cannot think otherwise, if we do not wish to trample upon all historical truth. If it is true, as the Greeks themselves relate regarding the origin of their mysteries, and as the Egyptians confirm—namely, that the Greek mysteries did indeed originate from the Egyptian ones (**)—then it is impossible, while attributing scientific knowledge to the Greek mysteries, to completely deny such knowledge to those from whom the Greeks received them. There is no reason why the Greeks, who were otherwise so jealous of everything that might bring them honor, would attribute the invention of the mysteries to the Egyptians if the invention actually belonged to themselves. Although they also relate that the mysteries were brought to them by **Ceres** <note>The Roman name for Demeter, the goddess of agriculture; the author refers here to the legend that she established the mysteries at Eleusis.</note>; but who does not see that this belongs to mythological reports?

<summary>The author argues that because the Greeks admitted their religious mysteries originated in Egypt, it is logical to assume the Egyptians possessed the same scientific and historical knowledge the Greeks are credited with. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the German historian Christoph Meiners, specifically challenging his skepticism regarding the depth of Egyptian theological knowledge compared to that of the Greeks.</meta>

<sig>z 2 VIII.</sig>

If the Greeks, who received their <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: "таинства"; referring to the secret religious rites and doctrines of antiquity.</note> from the Egyptians, are credited with offering sound concepts of God and revealing the one true God to their initiates, then this matter is effectively settled regarding the Egyptians as well. Everything that Meiners <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a philosopher and historian at the University of Göttingen known for his works on the history of religions.</note> argues against this in the 13th chapter of his history of religion is self-refuting; it is also contradicted by his own admission that the Egyptian priests taught the nature of the Deity in their mysteries—even if they did not possess a <term>natural theology</term> <note>A branch of theology that provides arguments for the existence and attributes of God based on reason and the observation of nature, rather than on divine revelation.</note> as pure as that which **Pythagoras** and certain others eventually brought out of Egypt.

This entire subject warrants a much more precise investigation, which would inevitably raise the question: ***What was the original religion of this people?*** However, such an inquiry would lead me too far from my intended purpose. Therefore, I shall proceed to other aspects of the great mysteries.

## VIII.

# Further Investigation of the Great Mysteries.

The doctrine of the existence of the most high God, the Creator and Ruler of the whole world, was in the closest connection with the doctrine of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "демоны"; in this context, the term refers to the Greek "daimon," meaning intermediate spirits or guiding divinities that bridge the gap between gods and humans, rather than "demons" in the modern sense of evil spirits.</note>, or intermediate beings. One could almost say that the teaching on the existence of such beings, who are situated as it were in the middle between God and men, is so natural that one cannot hear of the former doctrine without also calling to mind the latter. Wherever we turn in the history of the religions of ancient peoples, we find everywhere the accepted position that there are certain invisible beings, subject to the most high Being, and especially manifesting their action over this world.

That they taught about the nature of demons in the <term>mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world to which only initiates were admitted.</note> is also related by the ancients. And even if they had not declared this, one could very soon guess it with the greatest probability. The idea later adopted by the <term>Alexandrian philosophers</term> <note>A school of thought, including Neoplatonists like Plotinus, who flourished in Alexandria and sought to reconcile Greek philosophy with religious mysticism.</note>—that the most high Deity is a hidden being, keeping himself as it were always enclosed in his unity, without

<meta>catchword: with-</meta>

<summary>The author explains that ancient mysteries taught about intermediate spiritual beings (demons) who act as mediators between the supreme Creator and humanity. He suggests this hierarchical view of the divine was a natural and universal component of ancient religious thought, later refined by the Alexandrian philosophers.</summary>

<keywords>mysteries, demons, Alexandrian philosophers, supreme Deity, intermediate beings, theology, ancient religion, hierarchy of being</keywords>

doubt, was a fundamental tenet in the <term>mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world to which only initiates were admitted.</note>. Even in the most ancient times in Egypt, it was believed that the governance of this world was entrusted to certain invisible powers. And so, they naturally sought to provide the initiates with an understanding of these beings who were subordinate to the Most High God. From this arose, within the <term>sacred rites</term> <note>original: "таинства"; refers here to the secret teachings or sacraments of the mystery schools.</note>, the entire doctrine regarding the nature of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "демоны"; as previously noted, these are the "daimons" of Greek philosophy—spirits that bridge the gap between the divine and the human—rather than evil spirits in the modern sense.</note>, their various classes, their duties, and their different relationships to God and to men, between whom they formed a kind of middle ground.

Not everything narrated in <term>mythology</term> <note>original: "баснословіе"; literally "fable-telling," referring to the traditional myths and legends of the gods.</note> concerning the gods could be explained through the history of heroes; nor could everything be explained by the forces of nature. Certain deities of ancient peoples were, without doubt, neither deceased men nor personified objects of nature, but were in fact demons. This is how the pagan philosophers themselves explained their mythology of the gods, in order to give it a rational appearance. The <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>The influential Christian theologians of the first few centuries (such as Justin Martyr or Origen) who engaged in philosophical debates with the Greco-Roman world.</note> of the first centuries explained it in the same way during their disputes with the pagans, whom they repeatedly reproached with the claim that their gods were nothing other than demons. It is noteworthy that <term>paganism</term> <note>A term used by early Christians to describe the various non-Abrahamic, polytheistic religions of the Roman Empire.</note> was only completely overthrown when the <term>mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as those at Eleusis, which offered a more personal and "hidden" spiritual path than public civic religion.</note> were entirely eradicated. As long as they persisted, paganism always possessed an internal and very powerful reinforcement. For this reason, the Christians exerted every possible effort, not only to shake and undermine the reverence for them by disputing the mysteries, but also, during the time of **Constantine the Great**, to completely destroy them by force. One might say that in the second century, the ministers of the mysteries formed an alliance with the **Platonic philosophers** against Christianity.

Very closely connected with this teaching was the doctrine later taught by Plato, that human souls were in fact *demons* <note>original: "демоны"; in the Platonic and ancient sense, *daemons* were not evil spirits but intermediate beings or the divine essence within a person.</note>, and that the human body should be considered the grave of the soul, from which it must be liberated through various purifications and raised to bliss. Since Plato drew his primary teachings from the sources of the Egyptian priests and the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed in the transmigration of souls and the mathematical order of the universe.</note>, one might conclude from this that this teaching belonged to the great mysteries. But Plato himself declares that this opinion actually originates from the Orphics <note>Followers of Orphism, a set of religious beliefs and practices in the ancient Greek world associated with the mythical poet Orpheus.</note>, and he played on the words *soma* and *sema*, of which the first means *body*, and the second *grave* (*) <note>original: σῶμα (soma) and σῆμα (sema). This famous wordplay suggests the body is a tomb for the soul.</note>. Finally, Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), a Roman statesman and philosopher who frequently translated Greek philosophical concepts for a Roman audience.</note> numbers this teaching precisely among those taught by the most ancient prophets, leaders in the mysteries, and interpreters of divine revelations—

If one considers the great mysteries according to what I have said of them thus far, then everything offered in them had a direct relation to religion. But if we also remember that in those times all information, all scholarly knowledge belonged to the priests, and that the priesthood maintained its importance by establishing itself in the exclusive possession of all knowledge concerning the sciences: then we will easily see that not only religion constituted the subject of the great mysteries, but other forms of knowledge also belonged to it. Therefore, Clement of Alexandria <note>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150 – c. 215 AD) was a Christian theologian who often wrote about the relationship between Greek philosophy and religious truth.</note> says *that all things in general concerned the great mysteries, and that in them they came to know nature and its works* (*). This knowledge, on the one hand, was also connected with the revelations concerning religion, and just as mythology <term>Mythology: translated from the Russian 'баснословіе' (basnoslovie), which literally means 'the telling of fables,' referring here to the study of ancient myths.</term> upon just and precise investigation led to ancient history and the doctrine of demons <note>In the ancient world, "demons" (daimones) were not necessarily evil; they were viewed as guiding spirits or intermediary beings between the divine and the human.</note>, in the same way it naturally led to the knowledge of nature;

For certain deities were nothing other than the objects of nature represented in personified forms. Plutarch says the same, referring toward this end to the Egyptian fables and the poems of Orpheus <note>Orpheus was a legendary musician and prophet; the "Orphic" poems often dealt with the origins of the cosmos and the soul.</note>. Such were the cosmogonies <term>Cosmogony: a narrative or theory concerning the beginning and development of the universe, often through the birth of gods.</term> of the ancients in general. Heraclitus <note>Heraclitus of Ephesus (c. 535 – c. 475 BC) was a pre-Socratic philosopher famous for his ideas on cosmic change and the "Logos."</note> deposited his book *On Nature* in the temple of Diana of Ephesus <note>The Temple of Artemis, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</note>. Tatian <note>An early Christian writer and theologian of the 2nd century AD.</note> refers to it as a collection of poems; and from what is related of it by Clement of Alexandria, it is evident that its content concerned the mysteries. Had this book—like all ancient cosmogonies—not concerned the gods of the popular religion, there would have been no reason to keep it secret; for otherwise, the Greeks taught the sciences publicly.

However, it was not only the workings of nature that had a relation to and connection with religion that were the subject of the Great Mysteries, but natural history in general, astronomy, and everything belonging to them. One cannot think this of the Greeks; for among them, the sciences were open and universal. But regarding the Egyptians, it is beyond doubt; for although even on-

<page-num>127</page-num>

the Hermetic writings <note>A body of mystical and philosophical texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary fusion of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth.</note>, which Clement of Alexandria <note>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150–215 AD), a Christian theologian who integrated Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> mentions, and some of which contain moral prescriptions; these are also mentioned by Diogenes Laertius <note>A 3rd-century biographer of the Greek philosophers.</note> and Diodorus Siculus <note>A 1st-century BC Greek historian known for his monumental universal history, "Bibliotheca historica."</note> (*). <meta>This page continues the discussion on the moral dimension of ancient mysteries. Here, the author addresses the Platonic view of the body as a "tomb" and transitions into an examination of Persian Mithraism.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

...that this body is the tomb of the soul <note>original: "тѣло сіе есть гробъ души" — a reference to the Orphic and Platonic concept *soma sema*, suggesting the physical body is a prison or grave for the divine soul.</note>—this opinion produced in Platonism and among the hermits of the earliest times an excessive morality, which is contrary to human nature and society. Is it really possible that even this very teaching, offered in the mysteries, was merely intellectual knowledge and was not accompanied by any—though perhaps quite irregular—moral prescriptions? To think so is impossible. And so, it was necessary that certain rules of conduct were given to the *Epopts* <term>Epopt<note>original: "Эпоптамъ" — from the Greek *epoptes*, meaning "one who has seen." It refers to the highest grade of initiate in the Eleusinian Mysteries who had witnessed the final revelations.</note></term>. This is confirmed by the trials and preparations that preceded initiation. But how little we know of that! Meanwhile, however, even this small amount—these seemingly "dropped words"—are already sufficient to provide a fairly just concept of the mysteries of the ancients.

# IX.
## *On the Mysteries of Mithras among the Persians.*

Investigating with a certain precision the mysteries of the ancients, I cannot omit the so-called mysteries of Mithras among the Persians. Although I do not think that they were in any way connected to the Greek mysteries, they nonetheless deserve...

<summary>The author concludes that ancient mysteries must have included moral rules for high-level initiates (Epopts) and begins a new chapter investigating the Persian mysteries of Mithras.</summary>
<keywords>Platonism, soma sema, Epopts, moral philosophy, initiation rites, Mithras, Persian mysteries</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Mysteries of Mithras. The previous page ended by stating that while these Persian mysteries were likely independent of the Greek ones, they still deserve some attention.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>

...some attention. It is noteworthy that the ancients mention them, but in our modern discoveries nothing is found.

<margin>(*) Meiners in the aforementioned work, pp. 335-338. <note>original: "п. м." — abbreviation for "помянутомъ мѣстѣ" (loco citato). Christoph Meiners was a 18th-century German philosopher and historian known for his works on the history of religions.</note></margin>

Meiners thinks that the ancient Persians, until the time of Herodotus <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BC), the Greek historian often called the "Father of History."</note>, had no mysteries. Herodotus, Xenophon, and Strabo say nothing about mysteries among the Persians, and in Meiners’ opinion, they would have been entirely contrary to the disposition of the Persian government and religion. Herodotus represents the Persians as a wild and unenlightened people. They had neither temples nor altars; they worshiped the sun, the moon, fire, water, and the earth; and with such a disposition among a people who did not revere gods in human form, mysteries naturally could not have existed (*). <note>The author suggests that mysteries—which usually involved secret rituals and dramas centered on personified deities—could not develop in a culture that viewed the divine as natural elements rather than human-like figures.</note>

<margin>(**) Herodotus, Book I, p. 32.</margin>

However willingly I might agree with Herodotus in his narrative, it contains much that is contradictory and untrue. For sometimes he says that the Persians are altogether coarse and wild, knowing no luxury in their food but eating whatever comes their way, and never drinking wine, but drinking only water (**). Yet sometimes...

—on the contrary, he says that they drink a great deal of wine, and while intoxicated, they deliberate on matters of the greatest importance (*). <note>Herodotus famously claimed that the Persians would discuss important matters twice: once while drunk and once while sober.</note> They live without any laws; however, they already possess such civil institutions that lepers must be kept at a distance from their cities, and such foreigners should not even be allowed into Persian territories. Already in later times, they learned from the Assyrians and Arabs the worship of *Venus Urania*, whom they call *Mithra*. <note>Herodotus conflates the goddess Alilat/Mylitta with Mithra; however, Mithra is historically a male deity associated with the sun and covenants.</note> However, the name *Mithradates*, which was held by the shepherd by whom the young *Cyrus* was raised, already shows that the Persians revered the deity *Mishra* <note>Mishra is a variation of the name Mithra.</note> much earlier, and did not adopt this so-called *Venus Urania* from the Assyrians and Arabs. In general, one cannot read Herodotus's reports about the Persians without finding in them much that is unfair and hollow; to this belongs the claim that their Magi <term>Magi: the priestly class of ancient Persia, practitioners of Zoroastrianism</term> considered it a merit if they could kill many mosquitoes, snakes, ants, and reptiles; for they would slay everything indiscriminately with their own hands, except for humans and dogs. <note>This refers to the Zoroastrian practice of killing 'khrafstar'—creatures considered harmful or evil—though the author here treats Herodotus's description as an exaggeration.</note>

<sig>I 2</sig>

That the Persians did not have deified humans—mortals raised to the status of gods—as the Greeks, Romans, and other nations did, is something we can not only readily admit, but is beyond doubt. However, it does not follow from this that they could not have had <term>mysteries</term> <note>secret religious rites or initiations</note>. Without doubt, the Egyptians had no deified humans before they became acquainted with the Greeks; yet they not only had <term>mysteries</term>, but the Greeks even borrowed their own <term>mysteries</term> from them. Even if there were no mythological stories about gods that could provide better instruction for the <term>Epoptae</term> <note>original: *Epoptam*; from the Greek *epoptēs*, referring to those initiated into the highest grade of the secret mysteries</note>, the veneration of the sun, moon, and the elements already provided sufficient subject matter for <term>mysteries</term>—provided it is true that the Persians worshipped these objects of nature. Since Egyptian deities were explained in this way, and since in the Greek <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: *tainstva*, a term for sacred secrets or sacraments</note> they often resorted to this explanation for certain deities—claiming they were not actually gods, but merely personifications of natural forces—why could the same not have occurred among the Persians?

Indisputably, it would be far more significant if one were to say that the Persians were always worshippers of a single God, and worshipped neither humans, nor animals, nor natural objects. In a nation so [devoted] to re-

I stated at the beginning that <term>mysteries</term> were introduced in those places where the original religion—that is, **Deism** <note>the belief in a supreme creator based on reason rather than revelation</note>—had been driven out by the superstition of polytheism. But even this objection cannot hold ground. For although the Persians did not fall into polytheism, it is nevertheless known that in the most ancient times they venerated the Supreme God under the symbols of fire and the sun, which could have provided sufficient cause for <term>mysteries</term>. Furthermore, were the contradictions to popular religion really the sole content of ancient <term>mysteries</term>? Did not teachings about demons, about human souls and their future state, and in general all knowledge pertaining to the sciences, constitute an important subject of the <term>mysteries</term>?

**Herodotus** relates that the Persians worshipped **Venus Urania** <note>The "Celestial Venus," associated with the heavens</note> under the name **Mithra**, whom the Arabs called **Mylitta**. In the opinion of **Meiners** <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian of religions</note>, the Persians subsequently worshipped both the moon and the sun in human form; the latter under the name **Mithras** and in the likeness of a warrior dressed in the Persian fashion. Writers generally divide all Persian worship into two classes: namely, that followed by the ***Sabians*** <note>Also known as Sabianism, an ancient religious tradition often associated with the worship of stars and planets</note>, or worshippers of the planets, and that to which the ***Magi*** <note>The priestly caste of ancient Persia</note>, worshippers of fire (*), were devoted. Hyde <note>Thomas Hyde (1636–1703), an English orientalist and author of "De Religione Veterum Persarum," an influential work on Persian religion</note>, drawing from Greek and Arabian writers, proved that this veneration of fire was only symbolic (**); and the most recent discoveries made by Anquetil <note>Abraham-Hyacinthe Anquetil-Duperron (1731–1805), the French scholar who first brought the Zend-Avesta to Europe</note> confirm this everywhere. All writers also agree that the introduction of Magism into Persia is attributed to Zoroaster. To him is also attributed, most notably by Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher of the 3rd century AD</note> †), the establishment of the mysteries of ***Mithra***. This is quite probable, given the division made by Zoroaster among the Magi, whose lower classes were occupied with outward worship, while the higher classes were concerned with knowledge relating to the sciences, just as it was among the Egyptians. Therefore, the foundation of the mysteries of Mithra may have been an opposition to Sabiism, just as among the Greeks and Egyptians the Great Mysteries were opposed to popular superstition; and they ceased when, at last, the service of the planets was completely eradicated, and the ado— <note>The text cuts off here, continuing to the next page; the full word is 'adoration'</note>

...the adoration of fire became a universal faith in Persia, preserved even to our times by the ***Guebers*** <note>original: "Гаурами" (Gaury). The Guebers are the Zoroastrian community in Iran who maintained their ancient faith after the Islamic conquest.</note>.

After that time, as Persia became more well-known, the mysteries of ***Mithra*** <note>An ancient Persian deity of light and covenants whose mysteries became a major "soldier's religion" in the Roman Empire.</note> passed from the East to the West, and they still continued during the time of ***Plutarch*** (*) <note>A Greek-Roman philosopher and historian (c. 46 – 119 AD).</note>. But I think we would be greatly deceived if we were to judge their true and original nature by how they were celebrated in the West. This would be the same as if we wanted to judge the true nature of the Egyptian mysteries by how they were performed in Rome and other countries outside of Egypt.

We know very little about these mysteries; yet even this little is quite worthy of note. Initiation into them was preceded by certain rigorous trials, which surpassed everything known to us regarding all other ancient mysteries, though they essentially resembled one another. <page-num>138</page-num>

The mysteries themselves were celebrated in caves, which were either natural or man-made. This is related by Tertullian <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155 – c. 220 AD), a prolific early Christian author from Carthage.</note>, and Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 234 – c. 305 AD) who wrote extensively on the history of religion.</note> agrees with him, stating that Zoroaster <note>The ancient Iranian prophet and founder of Zoroastrianism.</note> was the first to dedicate a cave to Mithra. In these caves, the signs of the Zodiac, the elements, and various other symbols of nature were represented. Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347 – 420 AD), the theologian and historian best known for his translation of the Bible into Latin.</note> also mentions other marvelous images, but does not name them specifically (*). From Lampridius's <note>One of the traditional authors of the *Historia Augusta*, a collection of biographies of Roman Emperors.</note> account of the initiation of Emperor Commodus <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 180–192 AD) known for his participation in gladiatorial combat and his devotion to Eastern cults.</note> into these mysteries, it is evident that a formal battle took place during the ceremony; for on this occasion, the Emperor had the misfortune of killing a man (**). Herodotus <note>The ancient Greek historian (c. 484 – 425 BC) known as the "Father of History."</note> relates something similar regarding the Egyptian mysteries, in which the battles were also often quite bloody. There must have been some military element in these mysteries; for Tertullian says that during the initiation, while holding a sword before the initiate, they attempted to place a crown upon his head, which he was required to reject and say that Mithra alone is his crown. Tertullian also calls—

(**) Lampridius in *The Life of Commodus*, Chapter 9 <note>original: "Lampridius in vita Commodi. Cap. 9."</note>.

...calls the initiate a *soldier of Mithra* <note>original: "Mithræ miles"</note> (*). This is consistent with what is recounted by *Julius Firmicus* <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century lawyer and Christian convert who wrote "On the Error of Profane Religions."</note>, that people were initiated into these mysteries fully armed with a shield, helmet, spear, and armor. Such attire would indeed be quite strange, and would serve as the first example of knights <note>The author uses the term "rytsari" (knights) here to liken the ancient warrior-initiates to the medieval knightly class familiar to his readers.</note> participating in the mysteries—the kind of men to whom the words of the Psalm always applied: *for I have not known the arts of learning* <note>original: "яко не познахъ книжная" (yako ne poznakh knizhnaya). This is a Church Slavonic phrasing of Psalm 70:15 (Septuagint numbering), often used historically to describe the warrior class's lack of formal education or literacy.</note>. But perhaps this occurred only before the initiation, and afterwards the knightly game was brought to an end. For *Porphyry*, who was undoubtedly well-instructed, recounts that the initiates were dressed in the forms of various beasts, in order to represent the doctrine of the transmigration of souls <term>transmigration: the belief that after death, a soul passes into another body, whether human or animal.</term>. This is all the more probable as it aligns with the teaching of *Zoroaster* regarding the soul's passage through the twelve signs of the Zodiac (**).

From *Tertullian* it is observed that a formal baptism was used during initiation into these mysteries, and it was believed that all sins were washed away by it
