sins of the past life; also, that signs were placed on the foreheads of the initiates, bread was offered in sacrifice, and the resurrection was figuratively represented (*). *Porphyry* <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the Mithraic mysteries in his work "On the Cave of the Nymphs."</note> adds to this that the hands and tongues of the initiates were anointed with honey, in order to purify them in this manner from all guilt.

*Meiners* <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian who wrote on the history of religions.</note> thinks to find in these features evidence that the mysteries of Mithra were established partly after the example of the Eleusinian mysteries <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually in honor of Demeter and Persephone.</note>, and partly after other rites characteristic of the followers of the Christian religion (**). But in my judgment, all this does not necessarily follow from that. It is true that some parts of them resemble the Eleusinian mysteries, but from this it does not follow at all that the Persians borrowed their mysteries from the Greeks, or modeled them after their example. These same elements are also found in the Egyptian mysteries. Fasting, abstinence, washings, and purifications are found in all the mysteries of the ancient world, whichever of them we might turn to. Some parts are even found among the *jugglers* <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, the term "jugglers" (Russian: жонглеры) was often used in ethnographic texts to describe shamans, medicine men, or ritual performers among indigenous peoples.</note> among the savage

Based on such similarities, it was previously thought that Nestorian Christians <term>Nestorian Christians: A branch of Christianity that spread through Central Asia and China along the Silk Road, often noted for their distinct theological views on the nature of Christ.</term> settled among the Tatars <note>In this period, "Tatars" was a broad term used by Europeans to describe various Turkic and Mongol peoples of Central and Northern Asia.</note>, and that the latter practiced the tonsure of priests, anointing with chrism <term>chrism: a consecrated oil used in religious ceremonies such as baptism or confirmation.</term>, and some kind of Lord’s Supper. But this practice was quite different, as is clearly evident. It is known and beyond doubt that the Christians introduced much from the pagan mysteries into their own practices.

## X.

### *On the Orphics and Pythagoreans.*

---

<margin>(*) Pausanias <note>Pausanias (c. 110 – 180 AD) was a Greek traveler and geographer whose writings provide crucial details on ancient Greek religious sites.</note>, Book I, chapter 30.</margin>
**Orpheus** is generally regarded in the ancient world as the father of the mysteries. At the very least, he was the poet of the mysteries; for in a great many ceremonies, Orphic hymns were sung during initiation. I omit here the entire investigation into who this Orpheus was. Suidas <note>original: "Свидасъ" (Svidas); referring to the Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> names seven of them. This entire investigation is, in some way, adjacent to the realm of fables. Meanwhile, it is generally thought of Orpheus the Thracian that he was the first to teach the Greeks the knowledge of the gods and the mysteries; and this universal opinion of the ancients is further confirmed by the fact that Orphic hymns were sung everywhere at the Greek mysteries (*). Aristotle, even in his own time, doubted...

However, his doubts cannot be preferred over the unanimous testimony of all the ancients; rather, it is observed from them only that undisputed historical truth, with the passage of time, always loses some of its credibility, until at last it becomes entirely unstable and doubtful. This same universal narrative of the ancients also proves that Orpheus brought the mysteries <term>mysteries: secret religious rites or "disciplina arcani" of the ancient world, often involving initiation and hidden knowledge.</term> out of Egypt into Greece and Thrace. Upon comparing Orpheus’s teaching with the ancient Egyptian, this narrative is greatly confirmed, although it cannot be denied that more recent Greek philosophers have greatly corrupted the original Orphic teaching.

The Greek mysteries, brought by Orpheus into Greece from Egypt, may indeed be regarded as the school of this ancient Mystagogue <term>mystagogue: from the Greek "mystagogos," a person who initiates others into secret religious rituals or explains sacred mysteries.</term>. But besides that, there were in the ancient world the **Orphics**, who are perhaps worthy of more precise investigation, or at least deserve it just as much and have as much similarity to the mysteries as the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, known for their focus on mathematics, music, and the transmigration of souls.</note>, of whom I shall soon speak.

Almost all the ancients who mention Orpheus narrate of him that

<page-num>145</page-num>

he was not only the inventor of mysteries among the Greeks and Thracians, but also the founder of a special sect, or school. In the times of Theseus, this sect existed among the Greeks, and it seems that it was swallowed up either by other Greek philosophical schools or by the Pythagorean school.

As much as can be concluded from the few remaining traces of it, the <term>Orphics</term> <note>Followers of the religious and philosophical teachings attributed to the mythical poet Orpheus.</note> introduced among themselves a certain way of life by which they differed from their contemporaries, and which is commonly called the <term>Orphic life</term> <note>An ascetic lifestyle practiced by devotees of the Orphic mysteries, emphasizing spiritual purity.</note>. From **Plato** it is observed that they abstained from eating anything that had life. They extended this so far that they did not even sacrifice animals to the gods, but offered them sacrifices of fruit, cakes, and honey (*). They considered any blood to be a kind of defilement, although humans might eat it, or although it might be shed upon the altars of the gods.

**Fraguier** <note>Claude-François Fraguier (1666–1728), a French scholar known for his works on classical antiquity.</note> thinks that <term>Orpheus</term> tried by this law to discourage his fellow countrymen from the custom of sacrificing humans to the gods and devouring

But when we recall that this was customary among all peoples who accepted the doctrine of the <term>transmigration of souls</term> <note>Also known as metempsychosis, the belief that after death, a soul begins a new life in a different body, human or animal.</note>, and that <term>Orpheus</term> drew all his teachings and institutions from Egypt, where the same doctrine was accepted: then we will soon see that nothing other than the belief in the transmigration of souls gave the <term>Orphics</term> cause for abstaining from the consumption of any meat.

In **Euripides'** <term>Hippolytus</term> <note>A tragedy by the Athenian playwright Euripides, first performed in 428 BCE.</note>, we find an accurate depiction of an Orphic in the person of **Hippolytus**, from which we can form fairly just ideas about what these mystics of deepest Greek antiquity were like. "*You are that one*," says **Theseus** to Hippolytus, "*that superior man who enjoys close association with the gods; you are the sage and the blameless. I will no longer believe your vain boasting. The gods would act quite foolishly if they had such close association with you. Deceive us further with your abstinence from meat, present yourself as <term>divinely inspired</term> <note>The original term "bogodukhnovennyy" implies one whose spirit is moved directly by God or the divine.</note> like a disciple of Orpheus, and boast of the vapors of many teachings, even as you are caught in the very act (**).*"

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the passage from Euripides' Hippolytus, interpreting the Orphic claim to divine contemplation and the sect's historical connection to Egyptian wisdom and Pythagorean thought.</meta>

From this passage, it is observed not only that the **Orphics** sought to distinguish themselves from other people by abstaining from meat-eating; but also that they wished to be regarded as purer and holier than others. From such superior purity, they promised themselves nothing less than the contemplation of the gods, or a closer association with them, which was generally and commonly called <term>Theoria</term> <note>Derived from the Greek *theoria*, meaning "beholding" or "spectacle." In this mystical context, it refers to a direct spiritual vision of the divine.</note>. Furthermore, one sees here in the person of the **Orphic** a man who boasts of knowing extraordinary sciences, and that the gods exert a special influence over him and honor him with their inspirations.

All these traits clearly demonstrate the Egyptian origin of this **Orphic** sect, and from this, one can already gain an understanding of those sciences in whose knowledge the **Orphics** took pride. I am inclined to believe the remark of **Mr. Heyne** <note>Christian Gottlob Heyne (1729–1812), a German classical scholar who was a pioneer in the scientific study of Greek mythology.</note> regarding this passage in **Euripides**, that the propositions **Euripides** attributes to **Hippolytus** had no other sources than the **Pythagorean** philosophy (*). Nevertheless, **Euripides** presents an **Orphic** here, not a **Pythagorean**, who could not have existed in the time of **Hippolytus**, and whom, having presented—

...the poet would have committed a chronological error <note>The author is finishing the thought from the previous page: if Euripides had placed a Pythagorean in the time of Hippolytus, it would be an anachronism; by using an Orphic, he maintains historical consistency while still referencing Pythagorean ideas.</note>. <margin>*) Iamblichus, *On the Life of Pythagoras*, p. 135.</margin>

Another mysterious school among the ancients is comprised of those known as the **Pythagoreans**. I do not associate them with the **Orphics** merely because both groups derived their teachings and customs from the **Egyptians**, but also because the **Pythagoreans** actually presented their own doctrines as being those of **Orpheus**. Not only does **Pythagoras** state in the works of **Iamblichus** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a famous biography of Pythagoras.</note> that he was initiated by **Aglaophemus** (*) in **Libethra**, **Thrace**; but **Herodotus** also confirms this, stating that the **Bacchic** and **Orphic** rites were of the same—that is, **Egyptian**—origin as the **Pythagoreans** (**).

The accounts we possess regarding the life of **Pythagoras** are relayed with so many extraordinary circumstances that we are often tempted to regard them as mere fiction. **Heraclides Ponticus**, the first to chronicle his life, lived some two hundred years after him,

As <term>Iamblichus</term> <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote a famous biography of Pythagoras.</note> recounts, it was <term>Thales</term> <note>Often considered the first Greek philosopher and a mentor to Pythagoras.</note> himself who urged <term>Pythagoras</term> to travel to **Egypt** and there study the sciences with the **Memphis** and **Theban priests** (*). On this journey, he first traveled to **Phoenicia**, as this same writer relates, and was initiated in **Byblos** and **Tyre**. I will agree with <term>Meiners</term> <note>Christoph Meiners (1747–1810), a German philosopher and historian known for his skeptical and often controversial histories of religion and mankind.</note> (**) in the view that Pythagoras was a <term>devout visionary</term> <note>original: "набожной фантастъ" (nabozhnoy fantast) — implying someone whose religious devotion leads to mystical or imaginative extremes.</note> who set out in search of sacred adventures and crawled through every temple. But it does not follow from this that he cared more for superstition than for true sciences. For in whose possession was all knowledge regarding the sciences held at that time among the **Egyptians** and other similar nations, if not in the possession of the priests? Did not the <term>Great Mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world, often believed to hide advanced scientific or philosophical truths.</note> contain knowledge pertaining to the sciences, as Meiners himself writes?

Pythagoras spent a full twenty-two years in **Egypt**, and there benefited from the instruction of the **Memphis** and **Theban priests**, having endured all the difficult trials they usually imposed upon those to whom they wished to grant access to their <insert>mysteries</insert>—

It appears that upon entering the rank of the **listeners**, they brought a certain contribution which they gave to the society’s steward <note>original: "эконом" (steward/economos), the person responsible for the community's shared finances.</note>; it is likely that this supported the entire community. If, during the trials and preparations undertaken with them over the course of five years, they were found incapable of remaining in the society, or became unworthy of it in some other way: then their contribution was returned to them twofold. A monument was erected for them as if they had died, and from that time forward, they were regarded as completely deceased members; if they were encountered elsewhere, they were treated as strangers who had never belonged to the society (*).

It is not known exactly what the trials undertaken with the listeners during those five years consisted of; however, Iamblichus calls them <term>Orgiastic rites and initiations: *orgiasmous* and *myeiseis*. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the Pythagorean trials, specifically comparing them to ancient mysteries and illustrating the strength of their communal bond through the anecdote of a dying traveler and his recognition by a fellow member via secret signs.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>

...had a resemblance to the trials undertaken in the **Orgies** <note>original: "Оргіяхъ" (Orgii). In the ancient world, "orgies" referred to secret religious rites or "mysteries," such as those of Orpheus or Dionysus, involving sacred songs and purifications rather than the modern sense of the word.</note> and other mysteries.

It appears that the Pythagoreans had certain signs by which they recognized one another, and they were under a special obligation to help each other. One Pythagorean fell ill in a certain city far from his home, and at the time of his death, he found himself unable to pay his host for the care <note>original: "трудъ" (labor/effort), referring to the physical and financial burden of nursing the sick guest.</note> provided during his illness. Therefore, he gave him a pair of tablets and ordered them to be hung in a place in the city where every passerby could see them first. The host fulfilled the dying man’s wish, though he had no hope of success. But about a month later, another Pythagorean, passing through this place, noticed those tablets. He hurried to the host who had cared for his deceased brother, paid him generously, showered him with praise and gratitude, and by doing so, fulfilled what had been impossible for the deceased (*).

Such was the external organization of the Pythagorean sect. Their way of life,

<meta>This page continues the description of the Pythagorean lifestyle, focusing on their clothing, physical exercises, and specific dietary restrictions, drawing direct parallels with the customs of Egyptian priests to show the roots of their discipline.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>

conducted by the Pythagoreans in their assemblies, was entirely in the style of the **Egyptian priests**. They wore clothing made of **white linen**. They considered woolen garments to be impure, and for that reason, it was not permitted for them to wear such things. For them, the color **white** represented good, just as the color **black** represented evil (*). 

Going out on a hunt was strictly forbidden for them; on the contrary, they did not neglect other physical exercises, such as wrestling, running, ball-throwing, and others used by the ancients to strengthen the body; they also took walks every morning and every evening. 

In their entire way of life, they adhered to the diet <note>The word "diet" here reflects the Greek *diaita*, which refers to a whole lifestyle or regimen of health, rather than just food restrictions.</note> prescribed by Pythagoras, which was related in some way to his teachings. Their food consisted of bread, honey, fruit, and herbs; but **beans** <note>The Pythagorean prohibition of beans is one of the most famous and debated aspects of their tradition, variously explained as being due to their resemblance to embryos, their flatulent properties, or their association with the gates of Hades.</note> and all other such produce were completely excluded from it. All of this followed exactly the example of the Egyptians (**). Very rarely did they eat fish or sea plants. This, too, was adopted from the Egyptian discipline.

<meta>This page continues the description of the Pythagorean lifestyle, specifically focusing on their social isolation, hygiene rituals, and the comparison of their society to later monastic and philosophical orders.</meta>

But in the matter of drinking wine, Pythagoras departed from the aforementioned discipline, perhaps knowing that its prohibition among the Egyptians was purely political. <note>Historical sources often debated whether the Egyptian priestly ban on wine was for health, ritual purity, or state control; Pythagoras seems to have viewed it as a practical rather than a moral law.</note> However, the Pythagoreans drank wine only at dinner. They washed themselves daily; but they were not permitted to go to public baths where other people bathed, so as not to be defiled. From all of this, it is observed that Pythagoras everywhere sought to deprive his disciples of any connection with other people. This even extended to their worship; for they took no part in bloody sacrifices. <note>Pythagoras was famously opposed to animal sacrifice, a radical departure from standard Greek religious practice of the time.</note>

Such was the distinguished society established by Pythagoras. It is completely similar to a monastic community, and I think that it is not unjustly that the Pythagoreans are called the **Cartesians of Paganism**. <note>The author compares Pythagoreans to the followers of René Descartes (Cartesians), likely because both groups emphasized a rigorous, systematic, and almost mathematical approach to understanding the world and the soul.</note> Meanwhile, the external form does not make this institution unbelievable. In all times there have been people whose reason took a particular direction. Pythagoras had before him the example of the Egyptians, which he tried to imitate exactly; but since religion, civil institutions, and many other things prevented him from making his imitation completely equal to the original: so...

his society naturally could not take on any other form.

His teaching, communicated to his listeners, was symbolic, as I have already noted before this. It even extended to the moral precepts that he gave, several of which have been preserved for us by Diogenes Laertius, Iamblichus, and Porphyry. <note>These three authors are the primary biographers of Pythagoras from late antiquity; their accounts often mix historical fact with legend.</note> But the inner teaching of the Pythagoreans was essentially the same as that offered in the mysteries. <note>The "mysteries" refers to secret religious rites of ancient Greece, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were only open to initiates.</note> The theology of the Pythagoreans, much like that offered in the mysteries, was the opposite of the folk religion; for Pythagoras likewise taught the existence of a single, supreme Being, whom he called sometimes the **Monad**, sometimes the **Tetrad**, and sometimes the **Tetractys**, <note>original: "Monas, Tetras, Tetraktys." The Monad represents unity or the "One." The Tetrad and Tetractys refer to the number four and a triangular figure of ten points, which the Pythagoreans considered a sacred symbol of the universe.</note> and his own disciples relate that in his teaching about the gods, he borrowed some things from the Orphics, others from the Egyptian priests, from the Chaldeans and Magi, and also from the Eleusinian and Samothracian mysteries (*). From this, one can already conclude what kind of teachings he proposed. Moreover, he praised the veneration of gods and Heroes. This gives us the key to his entire teaching about demons, <note>In the ancient Greek sense, "demons" (*daimones*) were not necessarily evil, but were considered intermediary spirits or "divine sparks" existing between the gods and humanity.</note> and Iamblichus

### XI.
### On the Secret Method of Teaching.

**Pythagoras** presented his teachings through symbols; likewise, everything else written by the **Pythagoreans** was taught figuratively and secretly. In this regard, as in many others, **Pythagoras** was a faithful imitator of the **Egyptians**. The same reverence that the **Egyptians** and generally all peoples who possessed mysteries had for the secrets contained within them, the same rev—

This writing can naturally only continue as long as the sciences are in their most tender infancy, and as long as they concern themselves only with sensory objects that can be depicted. ...of a figurative image <note>Original: "наго изображенія" (the end of *obraznogo izobrazheniya*). This refers back to the "figurative writing" mentioned on the previous page.</note>, which would express an object either directly, or by means of a symbol, or similarity. These hieroglyphs naturally had to fall out of use as soon as they became insufficient for expressing everything. In their place, alphabetic writing was adopted by the Egyptians. But the same thing happened with it as in other cases. At first, a difficult <term>formal script</term> <note>Original: "уставной характеръ" (*ustavnoy kharakter*). In paleography, this refers to a majuscule or uncial hand, where letters are written separately and formally.</note> was used, which was later phased out among all nations by a <term>cursive hand</term> <note>Original: "косвеннымъ письмомъ" (*kosvennym pismom*), literally "slanting writing." On Greek and Roman coins and monuments, formal capital letters were used. The German cursive hand abolished the <term>Gothic monastic script</term> <note>Likely referring to "Blackletter" or "Fraktur" styles common in medieval manuscripts.</note>; however, the latter is still preserved not only on monuments but also in printed books. The same occurred with Egyptian hieroglyphs. They had to yield to letters, which were more convenient for expressing everything with ease.

...script; <note>This sentence completes the thought from the previous page regarding the retention of formal scripts.</note> however, they were retained for certain occasions, and because the oldest books of Egyptian learning were written with them, as the most ancient signs, they thus became the property of the priests. It is difficult to imagine that hieroglyphs originated in any other way; for it would be a great contradiction if we were to assume that the Egyptians invented their hieroglyphs specifically to hide their mysteries beneath them. This would be the same as trying to hide something from the people by presenting it clearly before their eyes. <note>The author argues that hieroglyphs were originally a common writing system that only became "secret" because the general public forgot how to read them as newer scripts evolved.</note>

...they still constituted a learned character, used for monuments and in sacred writings. The latter seems more probable to me; but I cannot expand upon this matter here. <note>This continues the author's argument from the previous page that hieroglyphs were once a standard script that transitioned into a specialized, "learned" form for formal use.</note>

In the time of Herodotus, two types of characters were known in Egypt. They use, he says, two kinds of letters, of which some are called sacred, and others common (*). <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BCE) distinguished between *hiera* (sacred) and *demotika* (popular/common) scripts.</note> It is noteworthy that Herodotus does not explain a single monument written in the sacred character. Perhaps even then their true meaning was largely unknown to the priests themselves.

<term>Warburton</term> <note>William Warburton (1698–1779), an English bishop and scholar whose work *The Divine Legation of Moses* included an influential study on the evolution of writing from pictures to alphabets.</note>, who reasons better than anyone regarding Egyptian Hieroglyphs, derives from what was said of them by <term>Porphyry</term> <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 CE) who wrote about the life of Pythagoras and Egyptian lore.</note> and <term>Clement of Alexandria</term> <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 CE) who provided one of the most detailed ancient descriptions of the various Egyptian scripts.</note> four distinct categories, namely:

1. **Proper Hieroglyphs**, which depicted objects exactly as they were.
2. **Symbolic writing**, in which, through some resemblance or comparison, invisible objects were expressed by means of sensory objects.
3. **Alphabetic writing**, which he calls the <term>Epistolary character</term> <note>Original: "Эпистолическій характеръ" (*Epistolicheskiy kharakter*). This refers to the cursive script used for letters and everyday documents, now known as Demotic.</note>.

4. Yet another secret priestly script, which he calls **Hierogrammatic**. <note>The "Hierogrammatic" script refers to the writing of sacred scribes, often used for religious texts.</note>

But upon closer investigation, this will appear entirely incorrect. The so-called symbolic writing had almost no difference from Hieroglyphs. In fact, it was nothing other than a supplement to those insufficient signs which, as the sciences expanded further, were not enough to express everything; it was nothing other than new images that expressed *immaterial* <note>The original Russian word *нечувственные* refers to objects or concepts that cannot be perceived by the five senses, such as "justice" or "the soul."</note> objects through resemblance. Although <term>Porphyry</term> <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> assumes a distinction between both these types of writing (\*); it is clear that he is mistaken in that. Regarding the fourth class—that is, the secret script—he mentions nothing at all. Only <term>Clement of Alexandria</term> <note>A prominent Christian theologian of the late 2nd century who provided a famous classification of Egyptian writing.</note> writes of four types of writing (\*\*). But it is evident that he was mistaken, regarding the so-called priestly character as a distinct style, just as he divides the Hieroglyphic script itself into the <term>Kyriologic</term> <note>From the Greek *kyriologikos*, meaning "speaking properly" or "literal." This refers to using a picture to represent the actual object shown.</note>, in which actual objects were depicted, and the **Enigmatic**.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Egyptian scripts, moving from the technical classification of characters (Hieroglyphic, Hieratic, etc.) to the symbolic and allegorical nature of Egyptian religious and scientific communication. The retention of the ancient writing style in works concerning the sciences eventually became a formal discipline among the Egyptians; anyone who wished to possess accurate and complete knowledge of the mysteries was first required to learn these written signs. Beyond this, it cannot be denied that the Egyptians also employed images when presenting their mysteries; just as they wrote with <term>Hieroglyphs</term> <note>Literally "sacred carvings," this script used pictures to represent both objects and sounds.</note>, they likewise concealed their teachings within riddles. They possessed not only figurative expressions under which they hid the truth, but also entire fables, or so-called "sacred stories" <note>original: *ἱερους λογους* (*hierous logous*)</note>, which contained meanings entirely different from their outward appearance. Regarding this, <term>Lucian</term> <note>Lucian of Samosata (c. 125–180 AD) was a Syrian-Greek satirist known for mocking hypocrisy and religious superstition.</note> says:

<margin>(*) Lucian, *The Assembly of the Gods*, Works Vol. III, p. 534, Reitz edition.</margin>

> *Indeed, what you recount regarding the Egyptians is unseemly; however, the majority of it consists of riddles, which do not deserve even the slightest mockery from the uninitiated <note>The original Russian *профановъ* (profanes) refers to those not initiated into the sacred mysteries.</note>.* (*)

<term>Plutarch</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–120 AD) whose work *On Isis and Osiris* is a primary source for Egyptian mythology as understood by the Greeks.</note> cites several of these enigmatic expressions; for instance, they call sea salt "Typhon’s foam," <note><term>Typhon</term> was the Greek name for the Egyptian god Set, associated with chaos and the desert.</note> wine "Typhon’s blood," a plumb line "the heart of <term>Bubastis</term>," <note>Bubastis refers to the goddess Bastet or her cult center; in this context, it likely refers to a specific ritual tool.</note> and saffron "the blood of [Hercules...]" <note>In the interpretatio graeca, the Egyptians often identified certain of their gods with the Greek Hercules.</note>

Hercules, and says that all their mythology was at its foundation nothing other than the history of nature, presented through images. <term>Apuleius</term> <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD) was a Roman writer and Platonist philosopher; his most famous work, *The Golden Ass*, describes an initiation into the mysteries of Isis.</note> was shown, during his initiation, a certain script with <term>Hieroglyphs</term> <note>From the Greek for "sacred carvings," this script used pictorial characters to represent both concepts and sounds.</note> and unknown characters.

This same symbolic and enigmatic method of presentation was borrowed from the <term>Egyptians</term> by <term>Orpheus</term> <note>A legendary Greek hero and musician whose "Orphic mysteries" were said to be derived from Egyptian rites.</note>, and transferred into his own mysteries. *The method of teaching truth by means of symbols*, says <term>Proclus</term> <note>A late Neoplatonist philosopher (412–485 AD) who sought to reconcile Greek philosophy with ancient religious traditions.</note>, *is characteristic of the Orphics and all who present the science of divine things under the husk of a fable* (*).

From there, <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>A 6th-century BC philosopher whose school combined mathematics, music, and mysticism.</note> also derived his entire symbolic doctrine. The Pythagoreans, says <term>Proclus</term> in another place (**), *were accustomed to offering allegories and symbols before the clear presentation of the sciences; after that, they would reveal the secret meaning of these symbols, and only after a sufficient purification of the mind and its preparation for the knowledge of truths, would they clearly present the entire doctrine in order.* <term>Porphyry</term> <note>A scholar and philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who wrote a biography of Pythagoras.</note>, <term>Iamblichus</term> <note>A Syrian Neoplatonist (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote extensively on Egyptian and Pythagorean mysteries.</note>, and <term>Diogenes Laertius</term> <note>A 3rd-century biographer of the Greek philosophers.</note> not only present the symbolic method of the <term>Egyptians'</term> teaching...

There can be no doubt that the great mysteries of the <term>Greeks</term> consisted not only of oral instruction, but that within them—just as with the <term>Egyptians</term> and other ancient peoples—there were secret writings. In the <term>Eleusinian temple</term> <note>A temple modeled after the famous site at Eleusis, where the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece were performed.</note> which the <term>Pheneatians</term> <note>Residents of Pheneus, an ancient city in northeastern Arcadia.</note> held in <term>Arcadia</term>, and in which they also celebrated the mysteries dedicated to **Ceres** <note>The Roman name for Demeter, the Greek goddess of grain and agriculture; the Eleusinian mysteries centered on her and her daughter Persephone.</note>, such sacred books were preserved within a hollow stone; during the Great Mysteries, the <term>Hierophants</term> <note>The high priests who "showed the sacred things" and interpreted the mysteries for the initiates.</note> taught the initiates from them (*). It is also evident that these books were written in such a way that only the <term>initiates</term> <note>Those who have been formally admitted into a secret society or religious mystery and sworn to secrecy.</note> could understand them. It seems that **hieroglyphs** belonged specifically to the Egyptians alone; yet symbolic teaching, fables, and riddles used to conceal the truth were almost universal among the ancients. <term>Hiram</term>, King of Tyre, and <term>Solomon</term> <note>The biblical King Solomon and King Hiram of Tyre are described by ancient historians (such as Josephus) as exchanging complex philosophical riddles to test each other's wisdom.</note> used to send riddles to one another to be solved.

Among the Church Fathers, one also finds many peculiar fragments attributed to the name of this poet. ## XII
### On the Decline of the Mysteries.

From the preceding, it is evident that <term>mysteries</term> existed in the ancient world and were held in universal esteem. Although their subject matter was very carefully hidden from all outsiders, it could not be so completely concealed that a few words were not dropped here and there. These words were collected, com-

<summary>This page discusses the attribution of various mystical hymns to Orpheus and Pythagoras, questioning their true authorship, and begins a new chapter on the eventual decline of the ancient mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, Pythagoras, Fabricius, authorship, Mystagogue, Decline of Mysteries, ancient secrets</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding how fragments of secret teachings were collected. The opening word "posed" completes the word "composed" from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>

posed into a whole from them, and finally it is perceived from this that the content of the <term>mysteries</term> <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Eleusinian or Mithraic mysteries, which required formal initiation.</note> was important, and that they were generally not unworthy of the respect held for them by so many great men in antiquity, and almost all <term>pagans</term> <note>The author uses this term in the historical sense to describe the followers of polytheistic religions in the Greco-Roman world.</note>. Now there remain further questions: *where did they disappear? when did they cease, and what provided the true cause for their cessation?*

This can be resolved quite quickly by answering that they, as pagan mysteries, ceased when the **Christian religion** became dominant in the **Roman Empire**. But that this answer is quite insufficient, and does not touch upon all the specific types of mysteries, requires no proof. It is known that certain mysteries were still preserved even when Christianity had already become the dominant religion, and after this time it is no longer known by what circumstance they were lost, such that today not even the slightest trace of them can be found. This deserves a more precise investigation, which perhaps will not be unpleasant to my readers. Such an investigation, when properly applied, can still bring much benefit even today.

In the case of certain **mysteries**, it is possible to determine the time of their cessation with a fair degree of certainty. The **Egyptian mysteries** suffered a significant disruption due to the decline of the authentic religion of the ancient Egyptians. This decline is usually dated to the times when the Egyptians were conquered by the Persian monarchs. However, in my opinion, this view is quite mistaken. Although **Cambyses** <note>Cambyses II (reigned 530–522 BC), the Persian King who conquered Egypt. Ancient sources often portray him as a mad tyrant who desecrated Egyptian shrines.</note> did indeed treat the Egyptians with great cruelty—slaying the animals they worshipped, plundering their temples, and ordering many treasures to be transported from them to Persia—this state of affairs did not last long. On the contrary, his successor **Darius** <note>Darius I, known as Darius the Great (reigned 522–486 BC), who was more respectful of local customs to ensure imperial stability.</note> proved himself a great patron and protector of the Egyptian religion, either because he possessed a deeper understanding of its true essence, or because he was motivated solely by political strategy. We ought to favor the former explanation if what is recounted by <margin>(*) Diodorus Siculus, Book I, p. 85.</margin> **Diodorus Siculus** <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BC; his "Library of History" is a major source for Egyptian lore.</note> is true: namely, that Darius maintained a close relationship with the Egyptian priests and was instructed in the contents of their sacred books (*). The latter reason, however, is likely because, in the opposite

case, it is incomprehensible how the Sovereign of the Persians, the sworn enemies of idol-worship <note>The Persians practiced Zoroastrianism, which avoided the use of cult statues, leading Greek and later historians to view them as inherently opposed to Egyptian "idolatry."</note>, could so greatly bolster Egyptian superstition that, according to the report of **Polyaenus** <note>Polyaenus was a 2nd-century CE Macedonian author known for his work "Stratagems in War."</note> (*), he promised a hundred <term>talents</term> <note>A talent was an ancient unit of mass for precious metals; a hundred talents represented an enormous sum of wealth.</note> to whoever should deliver a new **Apis** <note>Apis was the sacred bull of Memphis, considered a mediator between humans and the gods.</note>. **Jablonski** <note>Paul Ernst Jablonski (1693–1757) was a German Reformed theologian and orientalist who wrote extensively on Egyptian mythology.</note> thinks that the internal wars which troubled Egypt under the rule of the Persians provided the occasion for the decline of the mysteries. And where did the initiation take place? — In underground temples and passages, which in some places were established and were inaccessible to all outsiders. It seems likely to me that the decline of the **Egyptian mysteries** should be attributed only to the **Greeks**. From the time they acquired a certain respect for themselves in Egypt, the authentic religion underwent many transformations according to Greek taste, and already lost much of its originality. This was like a subtle poison, accompanied by a quiet illness. But the main cause of the decline of the mysteries was an important change suffered by the **priesthood** under the reign of the **Ptolemies** <note>The Ptolemaic dynasty was a Macedonian Greek royal house that ruled Egypt for nearly three centuries (305–30 BCE) following the conquests of Alexander the Great.</note>. These Sovereigns found the true way to keep Egypt in subjection and to extinguish the spirit of resentment toward foreign rule by taking away from the priests that influence which they had always held in state governance. For this reason, no **Greek** arriving in **Egypt** in these times for the purpose of obtaining instruction was subjected to such cruel trials as those proposed to **Pythagoras**; for it is likely that they did not dare to do so. But also, these **Greeks** did not penetrate deeply into the **Egyptian mysteries**. They attained only the most general knowledge. It is highly probable that the authentic **Egyptian mysteries** were then buried, or rather assumed a **Greek** dress; and beneath this new dress, the old one was so worn away that finally only a very few traces of it remained. When **Strabo** <note>Strabo (c. 64 BCE – 24 CE) was a Greek geographer and historian who traveled to Egypt during the Roman period.</note> lived in **Egypt** during the time of **Augustus** <note>Augustus Caesar, the first Roman Emperor, who annexed Egypt as a province in 30 BCE.</note>, there was no longer a single <term>Hierogrammatist</term> <note>original: "Іерограмматевса". There were then only the lowest degrees of mysteries, while the higher ones had long since ceased along with that generation which exclusively possessed them through so many centuries.

It seems that the school of the **Orphics** <note>Followers of Orphism, a set of religious beliefs associated with the mythical poet Orpheus, emphasizing asceticism and the soul's purification.</note> lasted for only a very short time among the **Greeks**. Although the specific circumstances that contributed to the destruction of the **Egyptian school** did not apply here, other causes made it impossible for such a society to endure for long. The **Orphic way of life**, characterized by abstinence from eating any animal creatures, stood in sharp contrast to the **Greek religion** and the customs of the **Greeks**. Among them, such austerity was so unusual that this society of exceptional people could not hope for any significant growth. Perhaps the ministers of the popular religion also played a part in oppressing these individuals. What the **Orphics** regarded as the highest degree of holiness—namely, the abstinence from bloody sacrifices—the public priests might have deemed as <term>impiety</term> <note>original: "безбожіе". In ancient Greek society, refusing to participate in animal sacrifice was often viewed as a rejection of the civic and religious order.</note>. Ultimately, this school was absorbed into the **Pythagoreans**, who wished to be recognized as nothing other than the followers of the **Orphics**.

The school of **Pythagoras** spread through **Lower Italy** <note>original: "Нижней Италіи". Referring to Magna Graecia, the coastal areas of Southern Italy settled by Greek colonists where Pythagoras established his community at Croton.</note> with great speed; however, conversely, it did not last long. Some believe that it was destroyed as early as the time of

...of **Pythagoras** himself, and that this philosopher was buried under the ruins of his own sect. But it is more likely that this happened after his death, and that the misfortune that befell the **Pythagoreans** occurred during the wars waged by **Dionysius of Syracuse** <note>Dionysius I (c. 432–367 BC) was a powerful Greek tyrant of Syracuse whose expansionist wars in Southern Italy often brought him into conflict with the cities where the Pythagoreans were influential.</note> against the **Crotonians**, when about 120 years had already passed since the founding of this sect.

Various reasons are cited for the destruction of the **Pythagoreans**. **Iamblichus** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–c. 325 AD) was a Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a major work on the life and teachings of Pythagoras.</note> relates that a certain man named *Cylon*, a wealthy and noble **Crotonian** of poor moral character, sought to be admitted into the society of the **Pythagoreans**; but having received a refusal, he afterwards tried, out of resentment, to destroy the **Pythagoreans** (*). This can be believed; for in all times there have been, *and even now there are people who grasp at what does not belong to them, and who allow themselves—either to achieve their improper goals or for revenge—all sorts of shameful acts, robbery, violation of hospitality, murder, and other atrocities.*

— **Iamblichus** continues that some, who were formerly **Pythagoreans** but were expelled from their society, and for whom a funeral monu- <note>The text cuts off mid-word (original: "надгроб-"). This refers to the Pythagorean custom of erecting a tomb or cenotaph for members who were expelled, symbolizing that they were dead to the community.</note>

(*) <note>original: "Iamblich. de vita Pythagoræ P. 199."</note> Iamblichus, *On the Life of Pythagoras*, p. 199.

After that time, there were also people among the Christians who were in fact devoted to the same principles that were offered in the mysteries, who respected their higher teaching and sought traces of them. But these had already been so extinguished and so thoroughly erased that it was impossible to restore them. That which was previously found in the temples upon monuments was buried in the ruins of the temples; that which was preserved by the philosophers was taken from the eyes of the world by their exile; and what still remained in certain places consisted only of faint traces, surrounded, moreover, by so many alien additions that even for an expert it would be difficult to precisely distinguish the authentic from the false.

Such was the fate of the mysteries of the ancient world, which once were held in universal respect, but afterwards were hidden so,

->**Decorative Binding and Endpaper**<-

This page presents a vibrant example of hand-marbled paper, a hallmark of traditional European and Slavic bookbinding. The design features a complex "nonpareil" <note>A term from the French word for "unrivaled," referring to a pattern of fine, combed lines.</note> or "snail" pattern, characterized by fluid, swirling shapes and delicate, feather-like details. 

The color palette consists of dusty blue, soft rose pink, pale yellow, and off-white. These patterns were achieved by floating pigments on a liquid base (often a "size" made of carrageenan or gum) and manipulating them with a comb or stylus before carefully dipping the paper to transfer the design.

On the right-hand side, a vertical strip of tan leather or parchment is visible. This is the structural hinge of the book, which secures the decorative endpaper to the binding’s spine and cover boards. The presence of such high-quality marbling and leather reinforcement suggests that this manuscript—a treatise on hermeneutics—was bound by a professional artisan for a library or a person of significant status.

<term>Marbling</term> <note>A decorative technique where colors are floated on a liquid surface to create patterns resembling stone or fluid movements; used extensively in high-quality bookbinding from the 17th century onward.</note>

<term>Endpaper</term> <note>Also known as "paste-downs," these are the sheets of paper at the very beginning and end of a book that are used to help secure the text block to the cover boards.</note>

<term>Hinge</term> <note>The flexible part of a book’s binding that allows the cover to open and close, often reinforced with leather or cloth for strength.</note>

# (STARCK, Baron J. A. von) — *On Ancient Mysteries* <note>original Russian transliteration: "O Drevnich Misterijach". Johann August von Starck (1741–1816) was a German theologian and a controversial figure in Freemasonry, known for his theories on the "Clerics of the Temple," an order he claimed descended from the medieval Knights Templar.</note>

...formerly existing among all peoples... <note>original Russian: "byvshikh u vsekh narodov"</note> Published at the expense of the Typographical Company in Moscow, at the free printing house, <note>The "Typographical Company" was a major publishing venture led by Nikolay Novikov, a central figure of the Russian Enlightenment and Freemasonry whose work was later suppressed by Catherine the Great.</note>

# (STARCK, Baron J. A. von) — *On Ancient Mysteries* <note>original Russian title: "O Drevnikh Misteriyakh". <meta>Exterior book cover / binding</meta>

The exterior of the volume features a weathered binding made of light brown material, likely leather or vellum. The surface is heavily mottled with dark spots and fine scratches, bearing the marks of significant age. Along the right edge, the spine is visible, showing raised bands and a small red leather title label. Though the gold lettering on the label has faded into illegibility, the spine remains structurally intact despite the wear.
